, i.e.,
S^{T}S=A_{0}
.

Remark 7.2
.

As argued from (
5.8
) to (
5.10
), this implies that the Radon-Nykodym derivative
\frac{d\widetilde{\omega}^{p}}{d\sigma}
satisfies the following
L\log L(d\sigma)
type estimate

-\infty<C^{\prime}(\lambda,\kappa,{\rm diam}\,\partial\Omega)\leq\int_{\partial\widetilde{\Omega}}\frac{d\widetilde{\omega}^{p}}{d\sigma}(\xi)\log\frac{d\widetilde{\omega}^{p}}{d\sigma}(\xi)\,d\sigma(\xi).

By symmetry, estimate (
7.1
) is equivalent to the existence of a constant
C=C(\lambda,\kappa,{\rm diam}\partial\Omega)
depending only on the ellipticity constant, the Lipschitz seminorm of the matrix
A
, and the diameter
{\rm diam}\,\partial\Omega
(but not on
\widetilde{\Omega}
) such that

(7.2)

\left|\int_{\partial\widetilde{\Omega}}\log|S\nabla g(\xi)|^{2}\,d\widetilde{\omega}_{T}^{p}(\xi)\right|\leq C<\infty,

where
S=A_{0}^{1/2}
, i.e.,
S^{T}S=A_{0}
,
g=g_{p}
is the Green function in
\widetilde{\Omega}
with respect to the matrix
A
with pole
p
, and
\widetilde{\omega}_{T}=\omega_{\widetilde{\Omega},A^{T}}^{p}
denotes the elliptic measure in
\widetilde{\Omega}
with respect to the matrix
A^{T}
and a pole
p\in\widetilde{\Omega}
such that
{\rm dist}(p,\partial\Omega)>r_{0}
. The existence of the matrix
S
is granted by the fact that the symmetric matrix
A_{0}
is uniformly elliptic with the same ellipticity constant as
A
, and hence positive definite. In fact,
S=R^{T}\sqrt{B}R
, where
\sqrt{B}=(\delta_{i=j}\sqrt{b_{ij}})_{1\leq i,j\leq 2}
.

Throughout all this section, when dealing with terms within solid integrals we will use the following notation:

•

We write
h(x)={\mathcal{O}}(f(x))
to denote “
|h(x)|\leq Cf(x)
almost everywhere with respect to the Lebesgue measure”.

•

Given a Lipschitz function
h
, we write
|\nabla h(x)|\leq C
instead of “
|\nabla h(x)|\leq C
almost everywhere with respect to the Lebesgue measure”. Recall that Lipschitz functions are differentiable almost everywhere by Rademacher’s theorem, see
[
Mat95
, Theorem 7.3]
.

7.1.
Directional derivatives and the dual space

We introduce some extra notation regarding the directional derivatives.

Notation
.

Definition of directional derivatives.

•

The (vertical) vector
e_{i}\coloneqq(0,\ldots,0,\overset{\mathop{\hbox{\hskip 0.60289pt\hskip-0.60289pt\hbox{$\hbox{${\scriptscriptstyle i}$}$}\hskip-0.60289pt\hskip-1.75pt\raisebox{-1.28006pt}{\hbox{$\rule{0.0pt}{0.0pt}\rule{0.0pt}{0.0pt}\hbox{${\scriptscriptstyle\smile}$}$}}\hskip-1.75pt\hskip 0.60289pt}}}{1},0,\ldots,0)^{T}
has
1
in position
i
and
0
’s otherwise. In
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
there are only two such vectors, namely
e_{1}=(1,0)^{T}
and
e_{2}=(0,1)^{T}
.

•

R_{i}\coloneqq e_{i}^{T}\cdot R
corresponds to the
i
-th row of
R
.

•

The
\partial^{i}
-directional derivative
\partial^{i}\coloneqq\partial_{R^{T}_{i}}
(superscript) is defined as

\partial^{i}f\coloneqq\partial_{R^{T}_{i}}f=\langle\nabla f,R^{T}_{i}\rangle=R_{i}\cdot\nabla f.

Here
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
denotes the standard scalar product in
{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}
. The derivative
\partial_{i}=\partial_{e_{i}}
(subscript) is the usual one in the direction
e_{i}
.

•

The
R
-directional gradient
\nabla_{R}
is defined as

\nabla_{R}f\coloneqq R\cdot\nabla f=\left(\partial^{i}f\right)_{i=1}^{n+1}.

Remark 7.3
.

The directional derivative
\partial^{i}
preserves the usual properties in sums, products, and logarithms, i.e.,
\partial^{i}(f+g)=\partial^{i}f+\partial^{i}g
,
\partial^{i}(fg)=g\partial^{i}f+f\partial^{i}g
, and
\partial^{i}\log f=\frac{\partial^{i}f}{f}
.

Note that

(7.3)

\left|\partial^{ij}f\right|=\left|\partial^{i}(R_{j}\cdot\nabla f)\right|=\left|\partial^{i}R_{j}\cdot\nabla f+R_{j}\partial^{i}\nabla f\right|\lesssim|\nabla f|+\left|\nabla^{2}f\right|,

where we denote
|\nabla^{2}f|^{2}\coloneqq\sum_{i,j=1}^{n+1}(\partial_{i,j}f)^{2}
.

Since the directions
R_{i}
change depending on the point, the usual integration by parts formula does not apply. Instead we have the following formula.

Claim 7.4

(Integration by parts formula)
.

Let
U\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}
be an open set. For
f,h\in W^{1,2}(U)
, if
fh\in W_{0}^{1,2}(U)
then

(7.4)

\int_{U}\partial^{i}f(x)h(x)\,dx=-\int_{U}f(x)h(x)\operatorname{div}R^{T}_{i}(x)\,dx-\int_{U}f(x)\partial^{i}h(x)\,dx.

Proof.

The product rule gives
\partial^{i}f(x)h(x)=\partial^{i}(f(x)h(x))-f(x)\partial^{i}h(x)
. By definition of the directional derivative
\partial^{i}
we can write

\displaystyle\partial^{i}(f(x)h(x))

\displaystyle=R_{i}(x)\cdot\nabla(f(x)h(x))=\sum_{j=1}^{n+1}\partial_{j}(f(x)h(x))R_{i,j}(x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{n+1}\partial_{j}\left(f(x)h(x)R_{i,j}(x)\right)-f(x)h(x)\sum_{j=1}^{n+1}\partial_{j}R_{i,j}(x)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{div}(fhR^{T}_{i})(x)-f(x)h(x)\operatorname{div}R^{T}_{i}(x),

which imply

\int\partial^{i}f(x)h(x)\,dx=\int\operatorname{div}(fhR^{T}_{i})(x)\,dx-\int f(x)h(x)\operatorname{div}R^{T}_{i}(x)\,dx-\int f(x)\partial^{i}h(x)\,dx.

Using that
C^{\infty}_{c}(U)
is dense in
W_{0}^{1,2}(U)
and the divergence theorem, the first element in the right-hand side is
\int\operatorname{div}(fhR^{T}_{i})(x)\,dx=0
.
∎

Again, since the directions
R
depend on the point, the directional derivatives do not commute. Instead, the following formula relating
\partial^{\alpha,\beta}
and
\partial^{\beta,\alpha}
is available, where
\partial^{\alpha,\beta}\coloneqq\partial^{\alpha}\partial^{\beta}
. We refer to this as the ‘almost’-commutative property.

Claim 7.5
.

Let
U\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n+1}
be an open set and
f\in W^{2,2}(U)
. For
\alpha,\beta\in\{1,\ldots,n+1\}
,

\partial^{\alpha,\beta}f-\partial^{\beta,\alpha}f=(\partial^{\alpha}R_{\beta}-\partial^{\beta}R_{\alpha})\cdot\nabla f.

Proof.

By definition we have
\partial^{\beta}f=R_{\beta}\cdot\nabla f=\sum_{i=1}^{n+1}\partial_{i}fR_{\beta,i}
. Applying
\partial^{\alpha}
gives

\partial^{\alpha,\beta}f=R_{\alpha}\cdot\nabla\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n+1}\partial_{i}fR_{\beta,i}\right)=\sum_{j=1}^{n+1}\partial_{j}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n+1}\partial_{i}fR_{\beta,i}\right)R_{\alpha,j}.

Expanding the derivative of this last expression and arranging we obtain

\partial^{\alpha,\beta}f=\sum_{i,j=1}^{n+1}\partial_{j,i}fR_{\beta,i}R_{\alpha,j}+\sum_{i=1}^{n+1}\partial_{i}f\sum_{j=1}^{n+1}\partial_{j}R_{\beta,i}R_{\alpha,j}.

The sum inside the second term in the right-hand side is precisely
\sum_{j=1}^{n+1}\partial_{j}R_{\beta,i}R_{\alpha,j}=R_{\alpha}\cdot\nabla R_{\beta,i}=\partial^{\alpha}R_{\beta,i}
. Hence

\partial^{\alpha,\beta}f=\sum_{i,j=1}^{n+1}\partial_{j,i}fR_{\beta,i}R_{\alpha,j}+\sum_{i=1}^{n+1}\partial_{i}f\partial^{\alpha}R_{\beta,i}=\sum_{i,j=1}^{n+1}\partial_{j,i}fR_{\beta,i}R_{\alpha,j}+\partial^{\alpha}R_{\beta}\cdot\nabla f.

By symmetry we obtain
\partial^{\beta,\alpha}f=\sum_{i,j=1}^{n+1}\partial_{j,i}fR_{\alpha,i}R_{\beta,j}+\partial^{\beta}R_{\alpha}\cdot\nabla f
, and subtracting
\partial^{\alpha,\beta}f-\partial^{\beta,\alpha}f
we get
Claim

7.5
.
∎

In particular, setting
\alpha=1
and
\beta=2
in the planar case we have

\partial^{1,2}f-\partial^{2,1}f=(\partial^{1}R_{2}-\partial^{2}R_{1})\cdot\nabla f,

and applying it to the Green function
g
, we get

(7.5)

\partial^{1,2}g-\partial^{2,1}g=(\partial^{1}R_{2}-\partial^{2}R_{1})\cdot\nabla g={\mathcal{O}}(|\nabla g|).

7.1.1.
Definition of the dual space

Let
U\subset\widetilde{\Omega}
and
u\in L^{1}_{\operatorname{loc}}(U)
. Define the linear functional

T_{u}(\psi)\coloneqq\int_{U}u\psi,

whenever it makes sense. In particular,
T_{u}\in L^{\infty}_{c}(U)^{\prime}
. To simplify the notation, we will also write
u(\psi)
to denote
T_{u}(\psi)
.

The
\partial_{i}
-derivative functional is defined as

T_{\partial_{i}u}(\psi)\coloneqq-\int_{U}u\partial_{i}\psi,

whenever it makes sense. Note that
T_{\partial_{i}u}\in W^{1,\infty}_{c}(U)^{\prime}
for
u\in L^{1}_{\operatorname{loc}}(U)
. Moreover, we also have
T_{\partial_{i}u}\in W^{1,2}_{c}(U)^{\prime}
whenever
u\in L^{2}_{\operatorname{loc}}(U)
. As before, we will also write
Du(\psi)
to denote
T_{Du}(\psi)
for any differential operator
D
. In general, the functional

T_{fD(u)}(\psi)\coloneqq T_{D(u)}(f\psi),

is in
W^{1,\infty}_{c}(U)^{\prime}
as long as either
f\in W^{1,2}_{\operatorname{loc}}(U)
and
u\in L^{2}_{\operatorname{loc}}(U)
, or
f\in W^{1,\infty}_{\operatorname{loc}}(U)
and
u\in L^{1}_{\operatorname{loc}}(U)
.

We claim that whenever
u\in L^{2}_{\operatorname{loc}}(U)
and
f\in W^{1,2}_{\operatorname{loc}}(U)
, in the dual space
W^{1,\infty}_{c}(U)^{\prime}
, the
\partial_{i}
-derivative functional satisfies the product rule

(7.6)

T_{\partial_{i}(fu)}=T_{\partial_{i}fu}+T_{f\partial_{i}u}.

Indeed, for any
\psi\in W_{c}^{1,\infty}(U)
, the previous equality reads as

-\int_{U}fu\partial_{i}\psi=\int_{U}\partial_{i}fu\psi-\int_{U}u\partial_{i}(f\psi),

which holds by the Leibniz’s rule a.e. for
W^{1,2}
-functions.

Using the definitions of
T_{\partial_{i}}
and the directional derivative
\partial^{i}(\cdot)=R_{i}\nabla(\cdot)
, see the beginning of
Section

7.1
, the directional
\partial^{i}
-derivative functional
T_{\partial^{i}u}:W^{1,\infty}_{c}(U)\to{\mathbb{R}}
, for
u\in L^{1}_{\operatorname{loc}}(U)
, is defined as

(7.7)

T_{\partial^{i}u}\coloneqq T_{R_{i}\nabla u}=\sum_{j}T_{R_{i,j}\partial_{j}u}.

Equivalently, for
\psi\in W^{1,\infty}_{c}(U)
,

(7.8)

\displaystyle T_{\partial^{i}u}(\psi)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j}T_{\partial_{j}u}(R_{i,j}\psi)=-\sum_{j}\int_{U}u\partial_{j}(R_{i,j}\psi)=-\sum_{j}\int_{U}u\psi\partial_{j}R_{i,j}-\sum_{j}\int_{U}uR_{i,j}\partial_{j}\psi

\displaystyle=-\int_{U}u\psi\operatorname{div}R_{i}^{T}-\int_{U}u\partial^{i}\psi.

This agrees with the integration by parts formula in (
7.4
) when
u\in W^{1,2}(U)
.

Next we claim that, in the dual space
W_{c}^{1,\infty}(U)^{\prime}
, for
u\in L^{2}_{\operatorname{loc}}(U)
and
f\in W^{1,2}_{\operatorname{loc}}(U)
, the directional
\partial^{i}
-derivative functional satisfies the product rule

(7.9)

T_{\partial^{i}(fu)}=T_{\partial^{i}fu}+T_{f\partial^{i}u}.

Indeed,
R_{i,j}\psi\in W_{c}^{1,\infty}(U)
when
\psi\in W^{1,\infty}_{c}(U)
, and hence, for
f\in W^{1,2}_{\operatorname{loc}}(U)
,

T_{\partial^{i}(fu)}(\psi)\overset{\text{{(\ref{def:distributional directional derivative})}}}{=}\sum_{j}T_{\partial_{j}(fu)}(R_{i,j}\psi)\overset{\text{{(\ref{product rule distributional final})}}}{=}\sum_{j}T_{\partial_{j}fu}(R_{i,j}\psi)+T_{f\partial_{j}u}(R_{i,j}\psi)\overset{\text{{(\ref{def:distributional directional derivative})}}}{=}T_{\partial^{i}fu}(\psi)+T_{f\partial^{i}u}(\psi),

as claimed.

Another important property is the following ‘almost’-commutative property of the directional derivatives (compare to
Claim

7.5
). For
u\in W^{1,1}_{\operatorname{loc}}(U)
, in the dual space
W^{1,\infty}_{c}(U)^{\prime}
, we claim that

(7.10)

T_{\partial^{1}(\partial^{2}u)}-T_{\partial^{2}(\partial^{1}u)}=T_{(\partial^{1}R_{2}-\partial^{2}R_{1})\cdot\nabla u}.

Indeed, for
\psi\in W^{1,\infty}_{c}(U)
and
\alpha,\beta\in\{1,2\}
,

T_{\partial^{\alpha}(\partial^{\beta}u)}(\psi)\overset{\text{{(\ref{def2:distributional directional derivative})}}}{=}-\int_{U}\partial^{\beta}u\psi\operatorname{div}R_{\alpha}^{T}-\int_{U}\partial^{\beta}u\partial^{\alpha}\psi.

Given
\{u_{k}\}_{k\geq 1}\subset C_{c}^{\infty}(U)
with
\lim_{k\to\infty}\|u_{k}-u\|_{W^{1,1}(\operatorname{supp}\psi)}=0
, this equals

(7.11)

\displaystyle T_{\partial^{\alpha}(\partial^{\beta}u)}(\psi)=

\displaystyle-\int_{U}\partial^{\beta}u_{k}\psi\operatorname{div}R_{\alpha}^{T}-\int_{U}\partial^{\beta}u_{k}\partial^{\alpha}\psi

\displaystyle-\int_{U}\partial^{\beta}(u-u_{k})\psi\operatorname{div}R_{\alpha}^{T}-\int_{U}\partial^{\beta}(u-u_{k})\partial^{\alpha}\psi.

By the integration by parts in (
7.4
) applied to
\partial^{\beta}u_{k}
, we have that the first row in the right-hand side is precisely
\int_{U}\partial^{\alpha}(\partial^{\beta}u_{k})\psi
. Applying now the ‘almost’-commutative property (
Claim

7.5
) here we obtain

\displaystyle T_{\partial^{\alpha}(\partial^{\beta}u)}(\psi)=

\displaystyle\int_{U}\partial^{\beta}(\partial^{\alpha}u_{k})\psi+\int_{U}((\partial^{\alpha}R_{\beta}-\partial^{\beta}R_{\alpha})\cdot\nabla u_{k})\psi

\displaystyle-\int_{U}\partial^{\beta}(u-u_{k})\psi\operatorname{div}R_{\alpha}^{T}-\int_{U}\partial^{\beta}(u-u_{k})\partial^{\alpha}\psi.

Again, by the integration by parts in (
7.4
), we can replace the first term in the right-hand side to obtain

\displaystyle T_{\partial^{\alpha}(\partial^{\beta}u)}(\psi)=

\displaystyle-\int_{U}\partial^{\alpha}u_{k}\psi\operatorname{div}R_{\beta}^{T}-\int_{U}\partial^{\alpha}u_{k}\partial^{\beta}\psi

\displaystyle+\int_{U}((\partial^{\alpha}R_{\beta}-\partial^{\beta}R_{\alpha})\cdot\nabla u_{k})\psi

\displaystyle-\int_{U}\partial^{\beta}(u-u_{k})\psi\operatorname{div}R_{\alpha}^{T}-\int_{U}\partial^{\beta}(u-u_{k})\partial^{\alpha}\psi.

Now, adding and subtracting
u
in the second row we get

\displaystyle T_{\partial^{\alpha}(\partial^{\beta}u)}(\psi)=

\displaystyle-\int_{U}\partial^{\alpha}u_{k}\psi\operatorname{div}R_{\beta}^{T}-\int_{U}\partial^{\alpha}u_{k}\partial^{\beta}\psi

\displaystyle+T_{(\partial^{\alpha}R_{\beta}-\partial^{\beta}R_{\alpha})\nabla u}(\psi)+\int_{U}((\partial^{\alpha}R_{\beta}-\partial^{\beta}R_{\alpha})\cdot\nabla(u_{k}-u))\psi,

\displaystyle-\int_{U}\partial^{\beta}(u-u_{k})\psi\operatorname{div}R_{\alpha}^{T}-\int_{U}\partial^{\beta}(u-u_{k})\partial^{\alpha}\psi.

By symmetry in (
7.11
) and subtracting we obtain

\displaystyle T_{\partial^{\alpha}(\partial^{\beta}u)}(\psi)-T_{\partial^{\beta}(\partial^{\alpha}u)}(\psi)=

\displaystyle T_{(\partial^{\alpha}R_{\beta}-\partial^{\beta}R_{\alpha})\nabla u}(\psi)+\int_{U}((\partial^{\alpha}R_{\beta}-\partial^{\beta}R_{\alpha})\cdot\nabla(u_{k}-u))\psi,

\displaystyle-\int_{U}\partial^{\beta}(u-u_{k})\psi\operatorname{div}R_{\alpha}^{T}-\int_{U}\partial^{\beta}(u-u_{k})\partial^{\alpha}\psi

\displaystyle+\int_{U}\partial^{\alpha}(u-u_{k})\psi\operatorname{div}R_{\beta}^{T}+\int_{U}\partial^{\alpha}(u-u_{k})\partial^{\beta}\psi.

By the convergence
\lim_{k\to\infty}\|u_{k}-u\|_{W^{1,1}(\operatorname{supp}\psi)}=0
we get (
7.10
).

7.1.2.
Properties of directional derivatives and the dual space

The following claim allows us to move from the initial matrix
A
to its symmetric part
A_{0}
, which we will relate to the directional derivatives in
Claim

7.7
.

Claim 7.6
.

Let
U\subset\widetilde{\Omega}
be an open set and
u\in W_{\operatorname{loc}}^{1,2}(U)
. Every
\psi\in W^{1,\infty}_{c}(U)
satisfies

\left(\operatorname{div}A\nabla u\right)(\psi)=\left(\operatorname{div}A_{0}\nabla u\right)(\psi)+\left(\sum_{i,j=1}^{n+1}\partial_{i}\left(\frac{a_{i,j}-a_{j,i}}{2}\right)\partial_{j}u\right)(\psi).

Proof.

Let
u\in C^{\infty}(U)
. By definition of the divergence and differentiating we have

\operatorname{div}A\nabla u=\sum_{i,j=1}^{n+1}\partial_{i}a_{i,j}\partial_{j}u+\sum_{i,j=1}^{n+1}a_{i,j}\partial_{i,j}u=\sum_{i,j=1}^{n+1}\partial_{i}a_{i,j}\partial_{j}u+\sum_{i,j=1}^{n+1}\frac{a_{i,j}+a_{j,i}}{2}\partial_{i,j}u.

Note that
a^{0}_{i,j}\coloneqq\frac{a_{i,j}+a_{j,i}}{2}
are precisely the coefficients of the symmetric matrix
A_{0}
. The product derivative rule gives

a^{0}_{i,j}\partial_{i,j}u=\partial_{i}\left(a^{0}_{i,j}\partial_{j}u\right)-\partial_{i}a^{0}_{i,j}\partial_{j}u,

and applying this relation to each pair of indexes
i,j\in\{1,\ldots,n+1\}
we get

\sum_{i,j=1}^{n+1}a^{0}_{i,j}\partial_{i,j}u=\operatorname{div}A_{0}\nabla u-\sum_{i,j=1}^{n+1}\partial_{i}a^{0}_{i,j}\partial_{j}u.

Summing up,

\displaystyle\operatorname{div}A\nabla u

\displaystyle=\operatorname{div}A_{0}\nabla u+\sum_{i,j=1}^{n+1}\partial_{i}a_{i,j}\partial_{j}u-\sum_{i,j=1}^{n+1}\partial_{i}\left(\frac{a_{i,j}+a_{j,i}}{2}\right)\partial_{j}u

\displaystyle=\operatorname{div}A_{0}\nabla u+\sum_{i,j=1}^{n+1}\partial_{i}\left(\frac{a_{i,j}-a_{j,i}}{2}\right)\partial_{j}u,

as claimed, for functions in
C^{\infty}(U)
.

Let us check this in the dual sense for a function
u\in W^{1,2}_{\operatorname{loc}}(U)
. Let
\psi\in W^{1,\infty}_{c}(U)
, and hence
u\in W^{1,2}(\operatorname{supp}\psi)
. Take
\{u_{k}\}_{k\geq 1}\subset C^{\infty}_{c}(U)
such that
\lim_{k\to\infty}\|u_{k}-u\|_{W^{1,2}(\operatorname{supp}\psi)}=0
. As we have the claim for each function
u_{k}
, in particular

\displaystyle\int_{U}A\nabla u\nabla\psi=

\displaystyle\int_{U}A\nabla(u-u_{k})\nabla\psi+\int_{U}A\nabla u_{k}\nabla\psi

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{U}A\nabla(u-u_{k})\nabla\psi+\int_{U}A_{0}\nabla u_{k}\nabla\psi-\int_{U}\sum_{i,j=1}^{n+1}\partial_{i}\left(\frac{a_{i,j}-a_{j,i}}{2}\right)\partial_{j}u_{k}\cdot\psi

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{U}A_{0}\nabla u\nabla\psi-\int_{U}\sum_{i,j=1}^{n+1}\partial_{i}\left(\frac{a_{i,j}-a_{j,i}}{2}\right)\partial_{j}u\cdot\psi

\displaystyle+\int_{U}A\nabla(u-u_{k})\nabla\psi+\int_{U}A_{0}\nabla(u_{k}-u)\nabla\psi-\int_{U}\sum_{i,j=1}^{n+1}\partial_{i}\left(\frac{a_{i,j}-a_{j,i}}{2}\right)\partial_{j}(u_{k}-u)\psi.

Now, since the matrix is Lipschitz and
\lim_{k\to\infty}\|u_{k}-u\|_{W^{1,2}(\operatorname{supp}\psi)}=0
, we conclude

\int_{U}A\nabla u\nabla\psi=\int_{U}A_{0}\nabla u\nabla\psi-\int_{U}\sum_{i,j=1}^{n+1}\partial_{i}\left(\frac{a_{i,j}-a_{j,i}}{2}\right)\partial_{j}u\cdot\psi,

as claimed.
∎

We treat one of the main terms in (
7.1
) by means of a perturbation argument. To do that, we note that we can write
\operatorname{div}(A_{0}\nabla u)
as in the diagonal case using the
R
-directional derivatives, plus an error term. More precisely, we have the following claim.

Claim 7.7
.

Let
A_{0}=R^{T}BR
satisfy the conditions in
Lemma

7.1
, let
U\subset\widetilde{\Omega}
be an open set and
u\in W^{1,2}_{\operatorname{loc}}(U)
. Every
\psi\in W^{1,\infty}_{c}(U)
satisfies

\left(\operatorname{div}(A_{0}\nabla u)\right)(\psi)=\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n+1}\partial^{i}(b_{i}\partial^{i}u)\right)(\psi)+\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n+1}b_{i}\partial^{i}u\cdot\operatorname{div}R^{T}_{i}\right)(\psi).

Proof.

Assume first
u\in C^{\infty}(U)
. Let us write
R\nabla u=(R_{j}\cdot\nabla u)_{j=1}^{n+1}=(\partial^{j}u)_{j=1}^{n+1}
, and so
BR\nabla u=(b_{j}\partial^{j}u)_{j=1}^{n+1}
. Hence,

R^{T}BR\nabla u=R^{T}(b_{j}\partial^{j}u)_{j=1}^{n+1}=\left(\sum_{j=1}^{n+1}R^{T}_{i,j}b_{j}\partial^{j}u\right)_{i=1}^{n+1}.

Therefore,

\operatorname{div}(A_{0}\nabla u)=\sum_{i=1}^{n+1}\partial_{i}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{n+1}R^{T}_{i,j}b_{j}\partial^{j}u\right)=\sum_{j=1}^{n+1}b_{j}\partial^{j}u\sum_{i=1}^{n+1}\partial_{i}(R_{j,i})+\sum_{j=1}^{n+1}\sum_{i=1}^{n+1}R_{j,i}\partial_{i}(b_{j}\partial^{j}u).

Note that the sum inside the first term in the right-hand side is precisely
\sum_{i=1}^{n+1}\partial_{i}(R_{j,i})=\operatorname{div}R^{T}_{j}
, and the sum inside the second term in the right-hand side is
\sum_{i=1}^{n+1}R_{j,i}\partial_{i}(b_{j}\partial^{j}u)=R_{j}\cdot\nabla(b_{j}\partial^{j}u)=\partial^{j}(b_{j}\partial^{j}u)
. Thus, the claim follows for
u\in C^{\infty}(U)
.

From the definition of the directional
\partial^{i}
-derivative in (
7.7
) and (
7.8
), the claim follows by a density argument as in
Claim

7.6
.
∎

7.2.
Sketch of the proof

Throughout this section the pole
p\in\Omega
so that
{\rm dist}(p,\partial\Omega)>r_{0}
is fixed unless it is otherwise stated (see
Lemma

7.10
below). In any case, it will be far from
\partial\Omega
and so from
\partial\widetilde{\Omega}
. Recall also that
S\coloneqq A_{0}^{1/2}
, i.e.,
S^{T}S=A_{0}
.

Fix
R\coloneqq\min\{1,r_{0}/2\}/2
, and so we have
0<R<\min\{1,r_{0}/2\}
. By
[
LLN08
, Lemma 3.35]
, see
Lemma

2.13
and
Remark

2.14
above, if
\widetilde{\Omega}
is Reifenberg flat enough, depending on the ellipticity constant
\lambda
and the value
\kappa\|A\|_{L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{n+1})}
, then there exists a constant
c=c(\lambda,\kappa\|A\|_{L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{n+1})})\geq 1
such that

c^{-1}|\nabla g(y)|\leq\frac{g(y)}{{\rm dist}\left(y,\partial\widetilde{\Omega}\right)}\leq c|\nabla g(y)|\text{ for every }y\in B(\xi,R/c)\text{ with }\xi\in\partial\widetilde{\Omega}.

Here we need to work with
(\delta,r_{0})
-Reifenberg flat domains with flatness parameter
\delta
smaller than some constant depending on the ellipticity constant
\lambda
and the value
\kappa\|A\|_{L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})}
.

Let us now fix the support function
\varphi
with
\varphi(p)=0
:

Remark 7.8
.

Let
\varphi\in C_{c}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})
with

•

\varphi=1
in
U_{\frac{R}{2000c}}(\partial\Omega)\coloneqq\{x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}:{\rm dist}(x,\partial\Omega)\leq\frac{R}{2000c}\}
,

•

\varphi=0
in
U_{\frac{R}{1500c}}(\partial\Omega)^{c}
, and

•

|\nabla\varphi|\lesssim 1
.

So
\operatorname{supp}\varphi\subseteq U_{\frac{R}{1500c}}(\partial\Omega)
. Note that
\partial\widetilde{\Omega}\subset\Omega\cap\operatorname{supp}\varphi
if we assume that
{\rm dist}_{\mathcal{H}}(\partial\widetilde{\Omega},\partial\Omega)
is small enough. With this choice of
\varphi
we have the comparability

(7.12)

|\nabla g(y)|\approx\frac{g(y)}{{\rm dist}(y,\partial\widetilde{\Omega})}\text{ for }y\in\widetilde{\Omega}\cap U_{\frac{R}{1500c}}(\partial\Omega)\supset\widetilde{\Omega}\cap\operatorname{supp}\varphi.

We claim

(7.13)

\log|S\nabla g|^{2}\in W^{1,2}_{\operatorname{loc}}(\widetilde{\Omega}\cap U_{\frac{R}{1500c}}(\partial\Omega)).

Indeed, this follows as
|\nabla g|\approx g/{\rm dist}(\cdot,\partial\widetilde{\Omega})>0
in
\widetilde{\Omega}\cap U_{\frac{R}{1500c}}(\partial\Omega)
and
g\in W^{2,2}(\widetilde{\Omega}\cap U_{\frac{R}{1500c}}(\partial\Omega))
, see (
7.20
).

Notation
.

From now on the variables and the region of integration will not be written unless they are not clear from the context.

Estimate (
7.2
) will follow from the following partial results.

For
a\geq 10
, let
\log_{(a)}x\coloneqq\max\{\log x,-a\}
. Note that in the weak sense
(\log_{(a)}(x))^{\prime}=\mathbf{1}_{\{x\geq e^{-a}\}}(x)\log x
.

Lemma 7.9

(Step 1)
.

For
a\geq 10
big enough,

\int_{\partial\widetilde{\Omega}}\log|S\nabla g|^{2}\,d\widetilde{\omega}_{T}^{p}=\int_{\partial\widetilde{\Omega}}\log_{(a)}|S\nabla g|^{2}\,d\widetilde{\omega}_{T}^{p}+\sigma(\partial\widetilde{\Omega}){\mathcal{O}}(ae^{-a/2}).

Since
\sigma(\partial\widetilde{\Omega})<\infty
, the second term in the right-hand side tends to zero as
a\to\infty
.

We prove the preceding lemma in
Section

7.5.1
.

Lemma 7.10

(Step 2)
.

For any
a\geq 10
so that
e^{-a}<\min_{z\in\operatorname{supp}\nabla\varphi}|S\nabla g(z)|^{2}
, and for a.e.
p\in\Omega
with
{\rm dist}(p,\partial\Omega)>r_{0}
,

\int_{\partial\widetilde{\Omega}}\log_{(a)}|S\nabla g|^{2}\,d\widetilde{\omega}_{T}^{p}=-\int_{\widetilde{\Omega}}\langle A^{T}\nabla\log_{(a)}|S\nabla g|^{2},\nabla(\varphi g)\rangle+{\mathcal{O}}\left(1+\int_{\operatorname{supp}\varphi}\frac{|\nabla^{2}g|}{|\nabla g|}g\right).

We prove the preceding lemma in
Section

7.5.2
. Note that the left-hand side integral is supported on the boundary, while the one on the right-hand side is a solid integral.

Next, fix
\widetilde{\Omega}_{\varphi}\subset\widetilde{\Omega}
a smooth domain satisfying

\widetilde{\Omega}\cap\operatorname{supp}\varphi\subset\widetilde{\Omega}_{\varphi}\subset U_{\frac{R}{1000c}}(\partial\Omega)\cap\widetilde{\Omega}.

With this choice we have the comparability
|\nabla g|\approx g/{\rm dist}(\cdot,\partial\widetilde{\Omega})
in
\widetilde{\Omega}_{\varphi}
, see (
7.12
). We prove the following two lemmas in
Section

7.5.1
.

Lemma 7.11

(Step 3)
.

For
a\geq 10
big enough,

\displaystyle-\int_{\widetilde{\Omega}}\langle A^{T}\nabla\log_{(a)}|S\nabla g|^{2},\nabla(\varphi g)\rangle=

\displaystyle-\int_{\widetilde{\Omega}}\langle A^{T}\nabla\log|S\nabla g|^{2},\nabla(\varphi g)\rangle

\displaystyle+{\mathcal{O}}\left({\mathcal{H}}^{2}(\Omega)e^{-a/2}+\int_{\widetilde{\Omega}\cap\operatorname{supp}\varphi\cap\{|S\nabla g|^{2}\leq e^{-a}\}}|\nabla^{2}g|\right).

Note that the last term in the right-hand side also tends to zero as
a\to\infty
because
g\in W^{2,2}(\widetilde{\Omega}_{\varphi})
and
\bigcap_{a\geq 10}\{|S\nabla g|^{2}\leq e^{-a}\}\cap\widetilde{\Omega}_{\varphi}=\emptyset
.

For
\varepsilon>0
as small as desired, we consider a given function
\psi_{\varepsilon}\in C^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})
satisfying

(1)

0\leq\psi_{\varepsilon}\leq 1
everywhere,

(2)

\psi_{\varepsilon}=0
in
U_{\varepsilon}(\partial\widetilde{\Omega})
,

(3)

\psi_{\varepsilon}=1
in
U_{3\varepsilon}(\partial\widetilde{\Omega})^{c}
, and

(4)

|\nabla\psi_{\varepsilon}|\lesssim\frac{1}{\varepsilon}
.

Lemma 7.12

(Step 4)
.

For
\psi_{\varepsilon}
as above we have

\int_{\widetilde{\Omega}}\langle A^{T}\nabla\log|S\nabla g|^{2},\nabla(\varphi g)\rangle=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\left\{\int_{\widetilde{\Omega}}\langle A^{T}\nabla\log|S\nabla g|^{2},\nabla(\psi_{\varepsilon}\varphi g)\rangle+{\mathcal{O}}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{U_{3\varepsilon}(\partial\widetilde{\Omega})}\frac{|\nabla^{2}g|}{|\nabla g|}g\right)\right\}.

In the weak sense, we can write the first term in the right-hand side in
Lemma

7.12
as
\operatorname{div}(A^{T}\nabla\log|S\nabla g|^{2})
acting on
\psi_{\varepsilon}\varphi g\in W_{c}^{1,\infty}(\widetilde{\Omega}_{\varphi})
, which can be understood as test functions. When studying the harmonic measure in the plane, i.e.,
A=Id
, this term is
0
by the harmonicity of
\log|\nabla g|
far from the critical points, a property that does not hold in general in higher dimensions. This was a key point to establish (
1.2
) for the Laplacian in
[
JW88
, Lemma 3.1]
.

In the following remark we discuss this argument in the constant coefficient case.

Remark 7.13

(Constant matrix and
L_{A}
-harmonic functions)
.

Suppose now that the matrix
A
is constant. Given any function
u
, by
Claim

7.6
,

\operatorname{div}(A^{T}\nabla u)=\operatorname{div}(A_{0}\nabla u),

i.e., we can reduce the study to the symmetric part. Moreover, by means of a linear change of variables, see
Lemma

3.4
, we have that
\operatorname{div}(A^{T}\nabla u)=0
if and only if
\widetilde{u}=u\circ D
satisfies
\operatorname{div}\left(\widetilde{A_{0}}\nabla\widetilde{u}\right)=0
where
\widetilde{A_{0}}=D^{-1}A_{0}(D^{-1})^{T}
, for any constant matrix
D
with
\det D\not=0
. Since
A
is constant now, if we choose
D=S^{T}
where
S=A_{0}^{1/2}
, i.e.,
S^{T}S=A_{0}
, then
\widetilde{A_{0}}=(S^{T})^{-1}A_{0}S^{-1}=(S^{T})^{-1}S^{T}SS^{-1}=Id
, and so
\Delta\widetilde{u}=0
. It is known that if
\Delta\widetilde{u}=0
, then
\Delta\log|\nabla\widetilde{u}|^{2}=0
(only) in the plane, whenever
\nabla\widetilde{u}\not=0
. If we undo the previous change of variables then we obtain

\operatorname{div}\left(A_{0}\nabla\left(\log\left|S\nabla u\right|^{2}\right)\right)=0,

off the critical points.

Back to the general case, when the matrix
A
is non-constant, instead of moving from our matrix to the Laplacian by means of a change of variables as it is done in the constant case (meaning that we would need a non-constant change of variables), we will work with its symmetric part. Using the particular form
A_{0}=R^{T}BR
, the rotation matrices will play the role of the directional derivatives, so we will be left with the diagonal matrix
B
, and this allows us to apply the previous strategy.

Now we study the first term appearing in the right-hand side in
Lemma

7.12
,

-\int_{\widetilde{\Omega}}\langle A^{T}\nabla\log|S\nabla g|^{2},\nabla(\psi_{\varepsilon}\varphi g)\rangle,

which is the most delicate, and the key point in the different behavior between the planar case and higher dimensions. Recall that, in the dual space, this term is

\left(\operatorname{div}(A^{T}\nabla\log|S\nabla g|^{2})\right)(\psi_{\varepsilon}\varphi g)\text{ where }\psi_{\varepsilon}\varphi g\in W^{1,\infty}_{c}(\widetilde{\Omega}_{\varphi}).

Notation
.

From now on, a boxed term
\boxed{X}
(representing the functional
T_{X}
) can be understood as a pointwise function, while a functional written as
T_{X}
needs to be understood strictly in the dual sense.

In the following lemma we decompose this last term.

Lemma 7.14

(Step 5)
.

In the dual space
W_{c}^{1,\infty}(\widetilde{\Omega}_{\varphi})^{\prime}
, we have

\operatorname{div}(A^{T}\nabla\log|S\nabla g|^{2})=\boxed{M1}+\boxed{M2}+T_{M3}+T_{E},

where

\displaystyle\boxed{M1}

\displaystyle\coloneqq-\sum_{i=1}^{2}\frac{4b_{i}}{|S\nabla g|^{4}}\langle\partial^{i}\nabla_{R}g,B\nabla_{R}g\rangle^{2},

\displaystyle\boxed{M2}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\sum_{i=1}^{2}\frac{2b_{i}}{|S\nabla g|^{2}}\langle\partial^{i}\nabla_{R}g,B\partial^{i}\nabla_{R}g\rangle,

\displaystyle T_{M3}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\frac{2}{|S\nabla g|^{2}}\left\langle\sum_{i=1}^{2}\partial^{i}\left(b_{i}\partial^{i}\nabla_{R}g\right),B\nabla_{R}g\right\rangle,

and
T_{E}
is an error term (involving derivatives of the matrix
A
) of the form

(7.14)

T_{E}=\sum_{i=1}^{2}\frac{1}{|S\nabla g|^{2}}\partial^{i}\left({\mathcal{O}}(|\nabla g|^{2})\right)+{\mathcal{O}}\left(1+\frac{|\nabla^{2}g|}{|\nabla g|}\right).

We prove the preceding lemma in
Section

7.5.3
.

Note that, using (
7.3
), we have that the two main terms satisfy

(7.15)

\left|\boxed{M1}\right|+\left|\boxed{M2}\right|\lesssim 1+\left(\frac{|\nabla^{2}g|}{|\nabla g|}\right)^{2},

which is bad for our purposes. A similar behavior occurs with the term
T_{M3}
. Instead, we need to exploit their cancellation, as illustrated in the constant matrix case:

Remark 7.15

(Constant matrix and main terms)
.

If the matrix
A
were constant, and hence
R
and
B
were constant as well, then we would have
T_{E}=0
. That is,

\operatorname{div}\left(A^{T}\nabla\log|S\nabla g|^{2}\right)=\boxed{M1}+\boxed{M2}+T_{M3}.

This suggests that these 3 terms are the main terms, and the others must be bounded error terms. Moreover, we have the following points:

•

The key point in the plane is that
\sum_{i=1}^{2}\partial^{i}(b_{i}\partial^{i}g)=0
would imply
b_{1}\partial^{1,1}g=-b_{2}\partial^{2,2}g
(off the critical points). One can show that, as a consequence, the main terms
[formula]
and
[formula]
would cancel each other in the sense
\boxed{M1}+\boxed{M2}=0
off the critical points of
g
.

•

Since the derivatives would commute, i.e.,
\partial^{1,2}=\partial^{2,1}
, we would have

(7.16)

\sum_{i=1}^{2}\partial^{i}\left(b_{i}\partial^{i}\nabla_{R}g\right)=\nabla_{R}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{2}\partial^{i}\left(b_{i}\partial^{i}g\right)\right)=\nabla_{R}(0)=0,

where we used that the Green function
g
satisfies
\sum_{i=1}^{2}\partial^{i}(b_{i}\partial^{i}g)=0
. This is why we call
T_{M3}
the “zero” term. In contrast to the previous point, here it is not needed that we are in the plane. However, we use that the Green function is
L_{A}
-harmonic, that is, this term is zero even in higher dimensions.

All in all, in the constant matrix case we would have

\operatorname{div}\left(A^{T}\nabla\log|S\nabla g|^{2}\right)=\boxed{M1}+\boxed{M2}+T_{M3}=0

off the critical points of
g
.

The strategy explained in the previous remark is, morally, what we will do to prove the following two lemmas.

Lemma 7.16

(Step 6)
.

Pointwise
\left|\boxed{M1}+\boxed{M2}\right|\lesssim 1+|\nabla^{2}g|/|\nabla g|
in
\widetilde{\Omega}_{\varphi}
.

We prove the preceding lemma in
Section

7.5.4
.

Lemma 7.17

(Step 7)
.

In the dual space
W_{c}^{1,\infty}(\widetilde{\Omega}_{\varphi})^{\prime}
, the functional
T_{M3}
is of the form

T_{M3}=\sum_{j=1}^{2}\frac{2b_{j}\partial^{j}g}{|S\nabla g|^{2}}\left({\mathcal{O}}(|\nabla g|)+{\mathcal{O}}(|\nabla^{2}g|)+\partial^{1}\left({\mathcal{O}}(|\nabla g|)\right)+\partial^{2}\left({\mathcal{O}}(|\nabla g|)\right)\right).

Recall that this equality must be understood at a functional level, see (
7.36
) below.

We prove the preceding lemma in
Section

7.5.5
.

In the following lemma, we bound the error terms that have appeared in the previous computations.

Lemma 7.18

(Step 8)
.

For small enough
\varepsilon>0
,

|T_{M3}(\psi_{\varepsilon}\varphi g)|+|T_{E}(\psi_{\varepsilon}\varphi g)|\lesssim 1+\int_{\operatorname{supp}\varphi}\frac{|\nabla^{2}g|}{|\nabla g|}g.

We prove the preceding lemma in
Section

7.5.6
.

Lemma 7.19

(Step 9)
.

\int_{\operatorname{supp}\varphi}\frac{|\nabla^{2}g|}{|\nabla g|}g\leq C<\infty,

and for small enough
\varepsilon>0
,
\int_{U_{\varepsilon}(\partial\widetilde{\Omega})}\frac{|\nabla^{2}g|}{|\nabla g|}g\lesssim\varepsilon
.

We prove the preceding lemma at the end of
Section

7.4
.

Using the previous lemmas we obtain (
7.2
).

Proof of (
7.2
).

Let
p\in\Omega
with
{\rm dist}(p,\partial\Omega)>r_{0}
so that
Lemma

7.10
holds for all
a>10
integer. By
Lemmas

7.9
,
7.10
and
7.11
we have

\left|\int_{\partial\widetilde{\Omega}}\log|S\nabla g|^{2}\,d\widetilde{\omega}_{T}^{p}\right|\leq\left|-\int\langle A^{T}\nabla\log|S\nabla g|^{2},\nabla(\varphi g)\rangle\right|+{\mathcal{O}}\left(1+\int_{\operatorname{supp}\varphi}\frac{|\nabla^{2}g|}{|\nabla g|}g\right).

By
Lemmas

7.12
,
7.14
,
7.16
and
7.18
, we get

\left|-\int\langle A^{T}\nabla\log|S\nabla g|^{2},\nabla(\varphi g)\rangle\right|\lesssim 1+\int_{\operatorname{supp}\varphi}\frac{|\nabla^{2}g|}{|\nabla g|}g+{\mathcal{O}}\left(\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{U_{3\varepsilon}(\partial\widetilde{\Omega})}\frac{|\nabla^{2}g|}{|\nabla g|}g\right).

In particular,

\left|\int_{\partial\widetilde{\Omega}}\log|S\nabla g|^{2}\,d\widetilde{\omega}_{T}^{p}\right|\lesssim 1+\int_{\operatorname{supp}\varphi}\frac{|\nabla^{2}g|}{|\nabla g|}g+{\mathcal{O}}\left(\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{U_{3\varepsilon}(\partial\widetilde{\Omega})}\frac{|\nabla^{2}g|}{|\nabla g|}g\right).

Finally, (
7.2
) follows from this and
Lemma

7.19
. Note that by Harnack inequality this is true for every
p\in\Omega
with
{\rm dist}(p,\partial\Omega)>r_{0}
.
∎

7.3.
Second derivatives of the Green function

In this subsection, we collect a fundamental property of the Green function in our situation, and some useful equalities and bounds that we will use later on involving the second order derivatives of the Green function.

The following claim points out the main difference between the planar and the higher dimensional case. This is a key point in the proof of
Lemmas

7.16
and
7.17
.

Claim 7.20
.

The Green function
g
satisfies

\sum_{i=1}^{2}\partial^{i}(b_{i}\partial^{i}g)=-\sum_{i=1}^{2}b_{i}\partial^{i}g\operatorname{div}R_{i}^{T}-\sum_{i,j=1}^{2}\partial_{i}\left(\frac{a_{ij}-a_{ji}}{2}\right)\partial_{j}g={\mathcal{O}}(|\nabla g|).

a.e. in
U_{r_{0}}(\partial\Omega)\cap\widetilde{\Omega}
. In particular,

(7.17)

b_{1}\partial^{1,1}g=-b_{2}\partial^{2,2}g+{\mathcal{O}}(|\nabla g|).

Proof.

By
Claims

7.7
and
7.6
,

\displaystyle\operatorname{div}A\nabla g

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{2}\partial^{i}(b_{i}\partial^{i}g)+\sum_{i=1}^{2}b_{i}\partial^{i}g\operatorname{div}R_{i}^{T}+\sum_{i,j=1}^{2}\partial_{i}\left(\frac{a_{ij}-a_{ji}}{2}\right)\partial_{j}g

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{2}\partial^{i}(b_{i}\partial^{i}g)+{\mathcal{O}}(|\nabla g|),

where we used that the matrices
A
,
B
and
R
are Lipschitz. The claim follows from the fact that the Green function satisfies
\operatorname{div}A\nabla g=0
a.e. in
U_{r_{0}}(\partial\Omega)\cap\widetilde{\Omega}
, see
Remark

2.11
.

As a consequence of
\partial^{i}(b_{i}\partial^{i}g)=\partial^{i}b_{i}\partial^{i}g+b_{i}\partial^{i,i}g
and
\partial^{i}b_{i}\partial^{i}g\in{\mathcal{O}}(|\nabla g|)
, we have (
7.17
).
∎

A close look to the proof reveals that if the matrix
A
were constant, then we would have
b_{1}\partial^{1,1}g=-b_{2}\partial^{2,2}g
in the planar case, as it was explained in
Remark

7.15
. Instead, we get the analog with some error terms in (
7.17
).

In the following claim we collect some basic equalities and estimates. These equalities will be useful in the decomposition procedure in
Lemma

7.14
.

Claim 7.21
.

Using the notation
\nabla_{R}f=R\cdot\nabla f
, see
Section

7.1
, we have

(7.18)

\partial^{i}|S\nabla g|^{2}=2\langle\partial^{i}\nabla_{R}g,B\nabla_{R}g\rangle+\langle\nabla_{R}g,\partial^{i}B\nabla_{R}g\rangle.

Consequently,

(7.19)

\partial^{i}\left(\frac{1}{|S\nabla g|^{2}}\right)=\frac{-\partial^{i}|S\nabla g|^{2}}{|S\nabla g|^{4}}\overset{\text{{(\ref{derivative product of dot product})}}}{=}-\frac{2\langle\partial^{i}\nabla_{R}g,B\nabla_{R}g\rangle+\langle\nabla_{R}g,\partial^{i}B\nabla_{R}g\rangle}{|S\nabla g|^{4}},

and

(7.20)

|\nabla\log|S\nabla g|^{2}|=\frac{|\nabla|S\nabla g|^{2}|}{|S\nabla g|^{2}}\lesssim 1+\frac{|\nabla^{2}g|}{|\nabla g|}\text{ in }\widetilde{\Omega}_{\varphi}.

Proof.

Since
S^{T}S=A_{0}
and
A_{0}=R^{T}BR
, we can write

|S\nabla g|^{2}=\langle S\nabla g,S\nabla g\rangle=\langle\nabla g,A_{0}\nabla g\rangle=\langle R\nabla g,BR\nabla g\rangle=\langle\nabla_{R}g,B\nabla_{R}g\rangle.

Recall that
R
and
B
are Lipschitz regular, thus we can apply the usual derivative rules a.e. to obtain

\partial^{i}|S\nabla g|^{2}=\langle\partial^{i}\nabla_{R}g,B\nabla_{R}g\rangle+\langle\nabla_{R}g,\partial^{i}B\nabla_{R}g\rangle+\langle\nabla_{R}g,B\partial^{i}\nabla_{R}g\rangle,

and (
7.18
) follows from the symmetry of
B
.

The inequality in (
7.20
) follows from

\displaystyle\left|\nabla|S\nabla g|^{2}\right|

\displaystyle{\approx}\sum_{i=1}^{2}\left|2\langle\partial^{i}\nabla_{R}g,B\nabla_{R}g\rangle+\langle\nabla_{R}g,\partial^{i}B\nabla_{R}g\rangle\right|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{2}2|\langle\partial^{i}\nabla_{R}g,B\nabla_{R}g\rangle|+\sum_{i=1}^{2}|\langle\nabla_{R}g,\partial^{i}B\nabla_{R}g\rangle|

\displaystyle\lesssim|\nabla g|^{2}+\sum_{i=1}^{2}|\partial^{i}\nabla_{R}g||\nabla g|\overset{\text{{(\ref{two directional derivatives})}}}{\lesssim}|\nabla g|^{2}+|\nabla g||\nabla^{2}g|,

and this concludes the proof of the claim.
∎

Remark 7.22
.

1/|S\nabla g|^{2}\in W^{1,2}_{\operatorname{loc}}(\widetilde{\Omega}_{\varphi})
because of (
7.19
),
|\nabla g|\approx g/{\rm dist}(\cdot,\partial\widetilde{\Omega})>0
in
\widetilde{\Omega}_{\varphi}
and
g\in W^{2,2}(\widetilde{\Omega}_{\varphi})
. By the same reason,
\log|S\nabla g|^{2}\in W^{1,2}_{\operatorname{loc}}(\widetilde{\Omega}_{\varphi})
.

7.4.
Whitney cubes and proof of Step 9

A classical way to compute an integral over a given set is to discretize it. We will do so using Whitney cubes, that is, we divide the domain into regions (cubes) which have diameter comparable to their distance to the boundary, so that Harnack’s and Caccioppoli’s inequalities can be used locally.

Let us start by defining the Whitney covering of
\widetilde{\Omega}
, and then we will move to study the properties of those cubes touching
\operatorname{supp}\varphi
.

Definition 7.23
.

There exists a collection
W_{\widetilde{\Omega}}
of dyadic cubes satisfying the following properties:

(W1)

\sqrt{2}\ell(Q)\leq{\rm dist}(Q,\partial\widetilde{\Omega})\leq 4\sqrt{2}\ell(Q)
, equivalently,
{\rm diam}Q\leq{\rm dist}(Q,\partial\widetilde{\Omega})\leq 4{\rm diam}Q
.

(W2)

1.5Q\cap\partial\widetilde{\Omega}=\emptyset
, since
{\rm dist}(1.5Q,\partial\widetilde{\Omega})\geq{\rm dist}(Q,\partial\widetilde{\Omega})-\sqrt{2}\cdot 0.25\ell(Q)\geq 0.75\sqrt{2}\ell(Q)>0
,

which we call
Whitney cubes
, see
[
Gra08
, Appendix J]
. We define
W_{\varphi}\coloneqq\{Q\in W_{\widetilde{\Omega}}:Q\cap\operatorname{supp}\varphi\not=\emptyset\}
.

Lemma 7.24
.

Every
Q\in W_{\varphi}
satisfies also:

(W3)

{\rm dist}(x,\partial\widetilde{\Omega})\leq 7\frac{R}{2000c}\leq R/c
for
x\in 1.5Q
.

(W4)

\ell(Q)\lesssim 1
.

(W5)

If
Q\cap\operatorname{supp}\nabla\varphi\not=\emptyset
, then
\ell(Q)\gtrsim 1
.

(W6)

For
x\in 1.5Q
we have

g(x)\approx g(A(\xi,10\ell(Q)))\lesssim\widetilde{\omega}_{T}(B(\xi,10\ell(Q)))\leq\widetilde{\omega}_{T}(50Q)\leq 1,

where
\xi\in\partial\widetilde{\Omega}
is such that
{\rm dist}(\xi,Q)={\rm dist}(\partial\widetilde{\Omega},Q)
, and
A(\xi,10\ell(Q))
is the Corkscrew point at
\xi
with radius
10\ell(Q)
.

Proof.

Note that for
x\in 1.5Q
, Harnack’s inequality gives
g(x)\approx g(A(\xi,10\ell(Q)))
. Using the relation between the elliptic measure and Green’s function in NTA domains (see
[
Ken94
, Lemma 1.3.3]
), we get

g(A(\xi,10\ell(Q)))\lesssim\widetilde{\omega}_{T}(B(\xi,10\ell(Q))).

Here we need
p\not\in B(\xi,3\cdot 10\ell(Q))
, which is granted as long as
\partial\widetilde{\Omega}
and
\partial\Omega
are close enough.

The remaining estimates in the lemma follow from the definition by standard arguments. The details are left to the reader.
∎

In order to bound the error terms arising in the proofs of
Section

7.2
, we will use (without mention) the following remark.

Remark 7.25
.

If
f\in{\mathcal{O}}(|\nabla g|^{k})
then
\left|\int\frac{f}{|\nabla g|^{k}}\varphi g\right|\leq C<\infty
.

Proof.

Since
g\lesssim 1
in
\operatorname{supp}\varphi
(see
Lemma

7.24
(
W6
)), we obtain

\left|\int\frac{f}{|\nabla g|^{k}}\varphi g\right|\leq\int\frac{|f|}{|\nabla g|^{k}}|\varphi g|\lesssim\int\left|\varphi g\right|\lesssim{\mathcal{H}}^{2}(\Omega)\leq({\rm diam}\partial\Omega)^{2}<\infty,

where we used that
\Omega
is bounded.
∎

Next we continue by controlling the Green function over Whitney cubes:

Lemma 7.26
.

We have:

(1)

\sum_{Q\in W_{\varphi}}\ell(Q)\cdot\widetilde{\omega}_{T}(50Q)\lesssim 1
.

(2)

For
\varepsilon>0
,

\sum_{\left\{Q\in W_{\widetilde{\Omega}}:Q\cap U_{\varepsilon}(\partial\widetilde{\Omega})\not=\emptyset\right\}}\ell(Q)\cdot\widetilde{\omega}_{T}(50Q)\lesssim\varepsilon
.

(3)

For
\varepsilon>0
,
\int_{U_{\varepsilon}(\partial\widetilde{\Omega})}g\lesssim\varepsilon^{2}
.

(4)

For each
Q\in W_{\varphi}
,
\left(\int_{1.5Q}g^{2}\right)^{1/2}\lesssim\ell(Q)\cdot\widetilde{\omega}_{T}(50Q)
.

(5)

For each
Q\in W_{\varphi}
,
\int_{Q}|\nabla g|\lesssim\ell(Q)\cdot\widetilde{\omega}_{T}(50Q)
.

(6)

For each
Q\in W_{\varphi}
,
\int_{Q}|\nabla^{2}g|\lesssim\widetilde{\omega}_{T}(50Q)
.

Proof.

Item

1
: As
\ell(Q)\lesssim 1
for every
Q\in W_{\varphi}
(see
Lemma

7.24
(
W4
)), there is
k_{0}
such that every
Q\in W_{\varphi}
satisfies
\ell(Q)=2^{-k}
with
k\geq k_{0}
. Note also that, for each scale
k\geq k_{0}
, the family
\{50Q\}_{\{Q\in W_{\varphi}\,:\,\ell(Q)=2^{-k}\}}
has finite overlapping. Therefore,

\displaystyle\sum_{Q\in W_{\varphi}}\ell(Q)\cdot\widetilde{\omega}_{T}(50Q)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\geq k_{0}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in W_{\varphi}\\
\ell(Q)=2^{-k}\end{subarray}}\ell(Q)\cdot\widetilde{\omega}_{T}(50{Q})=\sum_{k\geq k_{0}}2^{-k}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in W_{\varphi}\\
\ell(Q)=2^{-k}\end{subarray}}\widetilde{\omega}_{T}(50{Q})

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{k\geq k_{0}}2^{-k}\widetilde{\omega}_{T}\Biggl(\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in W_{\varphi}\\
\ell(Q)=2^{-k}\end{subarray}}50{Q}\Biggr)\leq\sum_{k\geq k_{0}}2^{-k}<\infty.

Item

2
: Recall that the cubes
Q\in W_{\widetilde{\Omega}}
with
Q\cap U_{\varepsilon}(\partial\widetilde{\Omega})\not=\emptyset
satisfy
\ell(Q)\lesssim\varepsilon
, and
\ell(Q)\approx\varepsilon
if
Q\cap(\partial U_{\varepsilon}(\partial\widetilde{\Omega})\setminus\partial\widetilde{\Omega})\not=\emptyset
, see (
W1
). Hence, as in the proof of
item

1
, taking
k_{0}(\varepsilon)\approx-\log_{2}\varepsilon
we have

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in W_{\widetilde{\Omega}}\\
Q\cap U_{\varepsilon}(\partial\widetilde{\Omega})\not=\emptyset\end{subarray}}\ell(Q)\widetilde{\omega}_{T}(50Q)\\
\leq\sum_{k\geq k_{0}(\varepsilon)}2^{-k}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in W_{\widetilde{\Omega}}\\
\ell(Q)=2^{-k}\end{subarray}}\widetilde{\omega}_{T}(50Q)\lesssim\sum_{k\geq k_{0}(\varepsilon)}2^{-k}\approx 2^{\log_{2}\varepsilon}=\varepsilon.

Item

3
: Using that
g\lesssim\widetilde{\omega}_{T}(50Q)
in
Q
, see (
W6
), that the cubes
Q\in W_{\widetilde{\Omega}}
with
Q\cap U_{\varepsilon}(\partial\widetilde{\Omega})\not=\emptyset
satisfy
\ell(Q)\lesssim\varepsilon
, see (
W1
), and
item

2
, we have

\int_{U_{\varepsilon}(\partial\widetilde{\Omega})}g\leq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in W_{\widetilde{\Omega}}\\
Q\cap U_{\varepsilon}(\partial\widetilde{\Omega})\not=\emptyset\end{subarray}}\int_{Q}g\overset{\text{{(\ref{green bounded by measure and 1 in the supp of varphi})},{(\ref{whitney p1})}}}{\lesssim}\varepsilon\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in W_{\widetilde{\Omega}}\\
Q\cap U_{\varepsilon}(\partial\widetilde{\Omega})\not=\emptyset\end{subarray}}\ell(Q)\widetilde{\omega}_{T}(50Q)\overset{\text{{(\ref{sum over whitney region p1 epsilon})}}}{\lesssim}\varepsilon^{2}.

Item

4
follows since
g(x)\lesssim\widetilde{\omega}_{T}(50Q)
for
x\in 1.5Q
(see
Lemma

7.24
(
W6
)).
Item

5
follows from Cauchy-Schwarz and Caccioppoli’s inequalities and
item

4
.

Item

6
: Since the Green function
g
of
\widetilde{\Omega}
is solution of
\operatorname{div}(A\nabla\cdot)=0
in
1.1Q
, the function
\widehat{g}(\cdot)=g(\ell(Q)\cdot)
is solution of
\operatorname{div}(\widehat{A}\nabla\cdot)
in
1.1\widehat{Q}
, where
\widehat{Q}\coloneqq\{x/\ell(Q):x\in Q\}
has side-length one, and
\widehat{A}(\cdot)=A(\ell(Q)\cdot)
. Moreover, since
\partial_{i}\partial_{j}\widehat{g}(\cdot)=\ell(Q)^{2}\partial_{i}\partial_{j}g(\ell(Q)\cdot)
, we get

\int_{Q}|\nabla^{2}g(x)|^{2}\,dx=\ell(Q)^{2}\int_{\widehat{Q}}|\nabla^{2}g(\ell(Q)x)|^{2}\,dx=\frac{1}{\ell(Q)^{2}}\int_{\widehat{Q}}|\nabla^{2}\widehat{g}(x)|^{2}\,dx.

The matrix
\widehat{A}
has the same ellipticity constant as
A
, but as
\ell(Q)\lesssim 1
for every
Q\in W_{\varphi}
, see
Lemma

7.24
(
W4
), the Lipschitz norm cannot grow too much:

\|\widehat{A}\|_{C^{0,1}\left(1.1\widehat{Q}\right)}\coloneqq\|\widehat{A}\|_{L^{\infty}\left(1.1\widehat{Q}\right)}+[\widehat{A}]_{C^{0,1}\left(1.1\widehat{Q}\right)}=\|A\|_{L^{\infty}(1.1Q)}+\ell(Q)[A]_{C^{0,1}(1.1Q)}\leq C\|A\|_{C^{0,1}\left(1.1Q\right)}.

Moreover,
{\rm dist}\left(\widehat{Q},(1.1\widehat{Q})^{c}\right)\approx 1
. Therefore we can apply (
2.2
) in
Theorem

2.9
to obtain

\int_{Q}|\nabla^{2}g|^{2}\,dx=\frac{1}{\ell(Q)^{2}}\int_{\widehat{Q}}|\nabla^{2}\widehat{g}|^{2}\,dx\overset{\text{{(\ref{cacciopoli type ineq for weak twice diff solutions})}}}{\lesssim}\frac{1}{\ell(Q)^{2}}\left[\left(\int_{1.1\widehat{Q}}|\nabla\widehat{g}|^{2}\right)^{1/2}+\left(\int_{1.1\widehat{Q}}\widehat{g}^{2}\right)^{1/2}\right]^{2}.

Applying Caccioppolli’s inequality and
item

4
, this implies

\int_{Q}|\nabla^{2}g|^{2}\,dx\lesssim\frac{1}{\ell(Q)^{2}}\int_{1.5\widehat{Q}}\widehat{g}^{2}=\frac{1}{\ell(Q)^{4}}\int_{1.5Q}g^{2}\overset{\text{{(\ref{sum over whitney region p2})}}}{\lesssim}\frac{\widetilde{\omega}_{T}(50Q)^{2}}{\ell(Q)^{2}}.

Using the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality and this, we get

\int_{Q}|\nabla^{2}g|\leq\ell(Q)\left(\int_{Q}|\nabla^{2}g|^{2}\right)^{1/2}\lesssim\widetilde{\omega}_{T}(50{Q}).

∎

Using this lemma we obtain
Lemma

7.19
.

Proof of
Lemma

7.19
.

Recall that, by (
7.12
) we have

|\nabla g(x)|\gtrsim\frac{g(x)}{{\rm dist}\left(x,\partial\widetilde{\Omega}\right)}\text{ for }x\in\operatorname{supp}\varphi,

provided the domain
\widetilde{\Omega}
is
(\delta,r_{0}/2)
-Reifenberg flat with
\delta
small enough depending only on
\lambda
and
\kappa\|A\|_{L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})}
. With this it suffices to show

\int_{\operatorname{supp}\varphi}|\nabla^{2}g(x)|{\rm dist}\left(x,\partial\widetilde{\Omega}\right)\,dx\leq C.

Summing in
W_{\varphi}
, by (
6
) and (
1
) in
Lemma

7.26
we obtain

\int_{\operatorname{supp}\varphi}|\nabla^{2}g(x)|{\rm dist}(x,\partial\Omega)\,dx\lesssim\sum_{Q\in W_{\varphi}}\ell(Q)\int_{Q}|\nabla^{2}g|\overset{\text{{(\ref{sum over whitney region p4})}}}{\lesssim}\sum_{Q\in W_{\varphi}}\ell(Q)\cdot\widetilde{\omega}_{T}(50{Q})\overset{\text{{(\ref{sum over whitney region p1})}}}{\lesssim}1,

as claimed.

Let us see the localized version. As above, using (
7.12
), and (
6
) and (
2
) in
Lemma

7.26
,

\displaystyle\int_{U_{\varepsilon}(\partial\widetilde{\Omega})}\frac{|\nabla^{2}g|}{|\nabla g|}g

\displaystyle{\lesssim}\int_{U_{\varepsilon}(\partial\widetilde{\Omega})}|\nabla^{2}g|{\rm dist}(x,\partial\widetilde{\Omega})\leq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in W_{\widetilde{\Omega}}\\
Q\cap U_{\varepsilon}(\partial\widetilde{\Omega})\not=\emptyset\end{subarray}}\ell(Q)\int_{Q}|\nabla^{2}g|

\displaystyle{\lesssim}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q\in W_{\widetilde{\Omega}}\\
Q\cap U_{\varepsilon}(\partial\widetilde{\Omega})\not=\emptyset\end{subarray}}\ell(Q)\widetilde{\omega}_{T}(50Q)\overset{\text{{(\ref{sum over whitney region p1 epsilon})}}}{\lesssim}\varepsilon.

∎

7.5.
Proof of Steps 1 to 8

In this subsection we prove remaining Steps 1 to 8 in
Section

7.2
.

7.5.1.
Proof of
Lemmas

7.9
,
7.11
and
7.12

Proof of
Lemma

7.9
.

We have

\left|\int_{\partial\widetilde{\Omega}}\log|S(\xi)\nabla g(\xi)|^{2}\,d\widetilde{\omega}_{T}^{p}(\xi)-\int_{\partial\widetilde{\Omega}}\log_{(a)}|S(\xi)\nabla g(\xi)|^{2}\,d\widetilde{\omega}_{T}^{p}(\xi)\right|\\
=\left|\int_{\{\xi\in\partial\widetilde{\Omega}:|S(\xi)\nabla g(\xi)|^{2}\leq e^{-a}\}}(\log|S(\xi)\nabla g(\xi)|^{2}+a)\,d\widetilde{\omega}_{T}^{p}(\xi)\right|

Since the domain is smooth, we can use (
2.8
) to write and bound the right-hand side term as

\displaystyle\Biggl|\int_{\{\xi\in\partial\widetilde{\Omega}:|S(\xi)\nabla g(\xi)|^{2}\leq e^{-a}\}}

\displaystyle-\langle A(\xi)\nabla g(\xi),\nu\rangle(\log|S(\xi)\nabla g(\xi)|^{2}+a)\,d\sigma(\xi)\Biggr|

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{\{\xi\in\partial\widetilde{\Omega}:|S(\xi)\nabla g(\xi)|^{2}\leq e^{-a}\}}|S(\xi)\nabla g(\xi)|(\left|\log|S(\xi)\nabla g(\xi)|\right|)\,d\sigma(\xi)

\displaystyle\leq\sigma(\partial\widetilde{\Omega})\left(e^{-a/2}|{\log e^{-a/2}}|\right).

∎

Proof of
Lemma

7.11
.

We have

-\int\langle A^{T}\nabla\log_{(a)}|S\nabla g|^{2},\nabla(\varphi g)\rangle+\int\langle A^{T}\nabla\log|S\nabla g|^{2},\nabla(\varphi g)\rangle\\
=\int\langle A^{T}\nabla\min\{0,a+\log|S\nabla g|^{2}\},\nabla(\varphi g)\rangle.

On the other hand, by (
7.12
) we have that if
a\geq 10
is big enough then
\varphi=1
in
\widetilde{\Omega}_{\varphi}\cap\{|S\nabla g|^{2}\leq e^{-a}\}
, and hence

\int\langle A^{T}\nabla\min\{0,a+\log|S\nabla g|^{2}\},\nabla(\varphi g)\rangle=\int\langle A^{T}\nabla\log|S\nabla g|^{2},\nabla g\rangle\mathbf{1}_{\{|S\nabla g|^{2}\leq e^{-a}\}}.

By (
7.20
), this is controlled in absolute value by

\int_{\{|S\nabla g|^{2}\leq e^{-a}\}}\left(1+\frac{|\nabla^{2}g|}{|\nabla g|}\right)|\nabla g|\leq\int_{\{|S\nabla g|^{2}\leq e^{-a}\}}|\nabla g|+\int_{\{|S\nabla g|^{2}\leq e^{-a}\}}|\nabla^{2}g|.

The lemma follows because the first term in the right-hand side is bounded by a constant times
{\mathcal{H}}^{2}(\Omega)e^{-a/2}
.
∎

Proof of
Lemma

7.12
.

For
\varepsilon>0
small we have
(1-\psi_{\varepsilon})\varphi=(1-\psi_{\varepsilon})
. Hence

-\int\langle A^{T}\nabla\log|S\nabla g|^{2},\nabla((1-\psi_{\varepsilon})\varphi g)\rangle=-\int\langle A^{T}\nabla\log|S\nabla g|^{2},\nabla((1-\psi_{\varepsilon})g)\rangle\\
=\int\langle A^{T}\nabla\log|S\nabla g|^{2},\nabla\psi_{\varepsilon}\rangle g-\int\langle A^{T}\nabla\log|S\nabla g|^{2},\nabla g\rangle(1-\psi_{\varepsilon}).

Using (
7.20
), the second term in the right-hand side is controlled in absolute value by

\int_{U_{3\varepsilon}(\partial\widetilde{\Omega})}\left(1+\frac{|\nabla^{2}g|}{|\nabla g|}\right)|\nabla g|=\int_{U_{3\varepsilon}(\partial\widetilde{\Omega})}|\nabla g|+\int_{U_{3\varepsilon}(\partial\widetilde{\Omega})}|\nabla^{2}g|\overset{\varepsilon\to 0}{\longrightarrow}0,

and the first term is controlled in absolute value by

\int_{U_{3\varepsilon}(\partial\widetilde{\Omega})}\left(1+\frac{|\nabla^{2}g|}{|\nabla g|}\right)g|\nabla\psi_{\varepsilon}|=\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{U_{3\varepsilon}(\partial\widetilde{\Omega})}g+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{U_{3\varepsilon}(\partial\widetilde{\Omega})}\frac{|\nabla^{2}g|}{|\nabla g|}g.

The lemma follows by applying (
3
) in
Lemma

7.26
in the last line.
∎

7.5.2.
Proof of
Lemma

7.10

Recall that the Green function
g
in
\widetilde{\Omega}
for the operator
L_{A}u=\operatorname{div}A\nabla u
satisfies, for every
\phi\in C^{0}(\overline{\widetilde{\Omega}})\cap W^{1,2}(\widetilde{\Omega})
,

(7.21)

\int_{\partial\widetilde{\Omega}}\phi(\xi)\,d\widetilde{\omega}_{T}^{x}(\xi)-\phi(x)=-\int_{\widetilde{\Omega}}\langle A^{T}(y)\nabla\phi(y),\nabla g_{x}(y)\rangle\,dy,\text{ for a.e.\ }x\in\widetilde{\Omega},

see (
2.4
).

We claim that

\varphi\log_{(a)}|S\nabla g|^{2}\in C^{0}(\overline{\widetilde{\Omega}})\cap W^{1,2}(\widetilde{\Omega}).

Indeed,
\varphi\log_{(a)}|S\nabla g|^{2}\in C^{0}(\overline{\widetilde{\Omega}})
because the function
\varphi
avoids the pole and
\nabla g
is continuous up to the boundary, see
Theorem

2.12
. Let us see that
\varphi\log_{(a)}|S\nabla g|^{2}\in W^{1,2}(\widetilde{\Omega})
. Using Jensen’s inequality as the function
x\mapsto\left|\log x\right|^{2}
is concave for
x>e
, the
L^{2}
-norm is controlled (depending on
a
) by

(7.22)

\displaystyle\int_{\widetilde{\Omega}\cap\operatorname{supp}\varphi}\left|\log_{(a)}|S\nabla g|^{2}\right|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\widetilde{\Omega}\cap\{|S\nabla g|^{2}\leq e\}}\left|\log_{(a)}|S\nabla g|^{2}\right|^{2}+\int_{\widetilde{\Omega}\cap\operatorname{supp}\varphi\cap\{|S\nabla g|^{2}>e\}}\left|\log|S\nabla g|^{2}\right|^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim{\mathcal{H}}^{2}(\widetilde{\Omega})\left(a^{2}+\left|\log\left(\;\mathchoice{{\vbox{\hbox{$\textstyle-$ }}\kern-7.57222pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.91281pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.30664pt}}{{\vbox{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle-$ }}\kern-5.13547pt}}\!\int_{\widetilde{\Omega}\cap\operatorname{supp}\varphi\cap\{|S\nabla g|^{2}>e\}}|S\nabla g|^{2}\right)\right|^{2}\right)<\infty,

which is bounded since
g\in W^{1,2}(\widetilde{\Omega}\cap\operatorname{supp}\varphi)
. The
L^{2}
norm of
\nabla(\varphi\log_{(a)}|S\nabla g|^{2})
is

\int_{\widetilde{\Omega}}|\nabla(\varphi\log_{(a)}|S\nabla g|^{2})|^{2}\leq\int_{\widetilde{\Omega}}|\nabla\varphi|^{2}\left|\log_{(a)}|S\nabla g|^{2}\right|^{2}+\int_{\widetilde{\Omega}}\varphi^{2}|\nabla\log_{(a)}|S\nabla g|^{2}|^{2}.

The first term is finite by (
7.22
) because
\varphi\in C^{\infty}_{c}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})
. From the definition of
\log_{(a)}x\coloneqq\max\{\log x,-a\}
and by (
7.20
), we get

|\nabla\log_{(a)}|S\nabla g|^{2}|\lesssim\mathbf{1}_{\{x\geq e^{-a}\}}(|S\nabla g|^{2})\left(1+\frac{|\nabla^{2}g|}{|\nabla g|}\right),

and hence the second term is controlled by

\int_{\widetilde{\Omega}\cap\operatorname{supp}\varphi}|\nabla\log_{(a)}|S\nabla g|^{2}|^{2}\lesssim\int_{\widetilde{\Omega}\cap\operatorname{supp}\varphi\cap\{|S\nabla g|^{2}\geq e^{-a}\}}1+\frac{|\nabla^{2}g|^{2}}{|\nabla g|^{2}}\lesssim{\mathcal{H}}^{2}(\widetilde{\Omega})+e^{a}\int_{\widetilde{\Omega}\cap\operatorname{supp}\varphi}|\nabla^{2}g|^{2}<\infty,

which is bounded as
g\in W^{2,2}(\widetilde{\Omega}\cap\operatorname{supp}\varphi)
.

By the choice of
\varphi
with
\varphi(p)=0
and
\varphi|_{\partial\widetilde{\Omega}}=1
, and plugging
\phi=\varphi\log_{(a)}|S\nabla g|^{2}\in C^{0}(\overline{\widetilde{\Omega}})\cap W^{1,2}(\widetilde{\Omega})
in (
7.21
), for a.e.
p\in\Omega
with
{\rm dist}(p,\partial\Omega)>r_{0}
we have

(7.23)

\displaystyle\int_{\partial\widetilde{\Omega}}\log_{(a)}|S\nabla g|^{2}\,d\widetilde{\omega}_{T}^{p}

\displaystyle=-\int_{\widetilde{\Omega}\cap\operatorname{supp}\varphi}\langle A^{T}\nabla\left(\varphi\log_{(a)}|S\nabla g|^{2}\right),\nabla g\rangle

\displaystyle=-\int\langle A^{T}\nabla\varphi,\nabla g\rangle\cdot\log_{(a)}|S\nabla g|^{2}-\int\langle A^{T}\nabla\log_{(a)}|S\nabla g|^{2},\varphi\nabla g\rangle

\displaystyle=\boxed{1}+\boxed{2}+\boxed{3},

with

\displaystyle\boxed{1}

\displaystyle\coloneqq-\int\langle A^{T}\nabla\varphi,\nabla g\rangle\cdot\log_{(a)}|S\nabla g|^{2},

\displaystyle\boxed{2}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\int\langle A^{T}\lx@nobreakspace\nabla\log_{(a)}|S\nabla g|^{2},\nabla\varphi\rangle\cdot g,\text{ and}

\displaystyle\boxed{3}

\displaystyle\coloneqq-\int\langle A^{T}\nabla\log_{(a)}|S\nabla g|^{2},\nabla(\varphi g)\rangle.

We claim that
\left|\boxed{1}\right|\lesssim 1
and
\left|\boxed{2}\right|\lesssim 1+\int_{\operatorname{supp}\varphi}\frac{|\nabla^{2}g|}{|\nabla g|}g
, and hence
Lemma

7.10
follows.

We start by controlling the term
\boxed{1}
. Recall that we have fixed
\varphi
so that
|\nabla\varphi|\lesssim 1
, and assumed that
a
is big enough so that
e^{-a}<\min_{z\in\operatorname{supp}\nabla\varphi}|S\nabla g(z)|^{2}
, that is,
\log_{(a)}|S\nabla g(z)|^{2}=\log|S\nabla g(z)|^{2}
for
z\in\operatorname{supp}\nabla\varphi
. Thus,

\left|\boxed{1}\right|\lesssim\int_{\operatorname{supp}\nabla\varphi}|\nabla g|\left|\log|S\nabla g|\right|\approx\int_{\operatorname{supp}\nabla\varphi}|S\nabla g|\left|\log|S\nabla g|\right|.

Since
\operatorname{supp}\nabla\varphi
is far from
\partial\widetilde{\Omega}
, in particular
{\rm dist}(x,\partial\widetilde{\Omega})\approx 1
for
x\in\operatorname{supp}\nabla\varphi
. Consequently, from this and (
7.12
) we have
|\nabla g|\approx g/{\rm dist}(\cdot,\partial\widetilde{\Omega})\approx g
in the support of
\nabla\varphi
, and hence

|S\nabla g(x)|\approx|\nabla g(x)|\approx g(x)\overset{\text{{(\ref{green bounded by measure and 1 in the supp of varphi})}}}{\lesssim}1\text{ for }x\in\operatorname{supp}\nabla\varphi.

Note that for
0<t<t_{0}
we have
t\left|\log t\right|\leq\max\{1,t_{0}\left|\log t_{0}\right|\}
. Therefore,

\boxed{1}\lesssim\int_{\operatorname{supp}\nabla\varphi}|S\nabla g|\left|\log|S\nabla g|\right|\lesssim{\mathcal{H}}^{2}(\operatorname{supp}\nabla\varphi)\leq C<\infty.

Now we study the term
\boxed{2}
. Since
\varphi
is fixed, we have
|\nabla\varphi|\lesssim 1
and so

\left|\boxed{2}\right|\lesssim\int_{\operatorname{supp}\nabla\varphi}g\cdot|\nabla\log_{(a)}|S\nabla g|^{2}|\leq\int_{\operatorname{supp}\varphi}g\cdot|\nabla\log_{(a)}|S\nabla g|^{2}|.

Plugging (
7.20
) in here we get

\left|\boxed{2}\right|\lesssim\int_{\operatorname{supp}\varphi}g+\int_{\operatorname{supp}\varphi}\frac{|\nabla^{2}g|}{|\nabla g|}g\overset{\text{{(\ref{green bounded by measure and 1 in the supp of varphi})}}}{\lesssim}1+\int_{\operatorname{supp}\varphi}\frac{|\nabla^{2}g|}{|\nabla g|}g.

∎

7.5.3.
Proof of
Lemma

7.14

In this subsection we study, via a perturbation argument, the functional term

\operatorname{div}\left(A^{T}\nabla\log|S\nabla g|^{2}\right)\in W^{1,\infty}_{c}(\widetilde{\Omega}_{\varphi})^{\prime}.

Note that the action of this functional on
\varphi g
gives rise to a modified version of the term
\boxed{3}
in (
7.23
).

First, we move from the matrix
A
to its symmetric part
A_{0}
by
Claim

7.6
, and we write its divergence in terms of the directional derivatives using
Claim

7.7
(see
Remark

7.22
):

\displaystyle\operatorname{div}\left(A^{T}\nabla\log|S\nabla g|^{2}\right)=

\displaystyle\operatorname{div}\left(A_{0}\nabla\log|S\nabla g|^{2}\right)+\sum_{i,j=1}^{2}\partial_{i}\left(\frac{a_{ij}^{T}-a_{ji}^{T}}{2}\right)\partial_{j}\log|S\nabla g|^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{2}\partial^{i}(b_{i}\partial^{i}(\log|S\nabla g|^{2}))+\sum_{i=1}^{2}b_{i}\partial^{i}(\log|S\nabla g|^{2})\operatorname{div}R^{T}_{i}

\displaystyle+\sum_{i,j=1}^{2}\partial_{i}\left(\frac{a_{ji}-a_{ij}}{2}\right)\partial_{j}(\log|S\nabla g|^{2})\eqqcolon T_{3.A}+\boxed{3.B}+\boxed{3.C}.

Note that the terms
[formula]
and
[formula]
contain derivatives of the matrix
A
. In particular, if the matrix were constant then these two terms would be zero, which suggests that the terms
[formula]
and
[formula]
must be bounded error terms.

Next we deal with
T_{3.A}
. As
1/|S\nabla g|^{2}\in W^{1,2}_{\operatorname{loc}}(\widetilde{\Omega}_{\varphi})
(see
Remark

7.22
), by (
7.9
) we have

(7.24)

\displaystyle T_{3.A}=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{2}\partial^{i}\left(b_{i}\frac{\partial^{i}|S\nabla g|^{2}}{|S\nabla g|^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{2}\partial^{i}\left(\frac{1}{|S\nabla g|^{2}}\right)b_{i}\partial^{i}|S\nabla g|^{2}+\sum_{i=1}^{2}\frac{1}{|S\nabla g|^{2}}\partial^{i}(b_{i}\partial^{i}|S\nabla g|^{2}).

We infer that each element in the sum in the first term in the right-hand side in (
7.24
) is

(7.25)

\partial^{i}\left(\frac{1}{|S\nabla g|^{2}}\right)b_{i}\partial^{i}|S\nabla g|^{2}\overset{\text{{(\ref{derivative quotient})}}}{=}\frac{-b_{i}\left(\partial^{i}|S\nabla g|^{2}\right)^{2}}{|S\nabla g|^{4}}.

Therefore, using identities (
7.18
) and (
7.25
) and expanding, the first term in the right-hand side of (
7.24
) can be written as

\sum_{i=1}^{2}\partial^{i}\left(\frac{1}{|S\nabla g|^{2}}\right)b_{i}\partial^{i}|S\nabla g|^{2}=\boxed{M1}+\boxed{E1}+\boxed{E2},

where

\displaystyle\boxed{M1}

\displaystyle\coloneqq-\sum_{i=1}^{2}\frac{4b_{i}}{|S\nabla g|^{4}}\langle\partial^{i}\nabla_{R}g,B\nabla_{R}g\rangle^{2},

\displaystyle\boxed{E1}

\displaystyle\coloneqq-\sum_{i=1}^{2}\frac{4b_{i}}{|S\nabla g|^{4}}\langle\partial^{i}\nabla_{R}g,B\nabla_{R}g\rangle\langle\nabla_{R}g,\partial^{i}B\nabla_{R}g\rangle,\text{ and}

\displaystyle\boxed{E2}

\displaystyle\coloneqq-\sum_{i=1}^{2}\frac{b_{i}}{|S\nabla g|^{4}}\langle\nabla_{R}g,\partial^{i}B\nabla_{R}g\rangle^{2}.

Note that
[formula]
and
[formula]
can be considered “error terms” from a perturbation point of view, since in case
B
were constant we would get
\boxed{E1}=\boxed{E2}=0
, while
[formula]
can be considered a “main term”.

Next we perform a similar decomposition for
\partial^{i}(b_{i}\partial^{i}|S\nabla g|^{2})
, in the second term in the right-hand side in (
7.24
). In this case, we need to pay attention to the first term in the right-hand side of (
7.18
):

(7.26)

\partial^{i}\left(b_{i}\langle\partial^{i}\nabla_{R}g,B\nabla_{R}g\rangle\right)=\left\langle\partial^{i}\left(b_{i}\partial^{i}\nabla_{R}g\right),B\nabla_{R}g\right\rangle+b_{i}\langle\partial^{i}\nabla_{R}g,\partial^{i}B\nabla_{R}g\rangle+b_{i}\langle\partial^{i}\nabla_{R}g,B\partial^{i}\nabla_{R}g\rangle.

Then using (
7.18
) and (
7.26
) we get that the second term in the right-hand side of (
7.24
) can be written as

\sum_{i=1}^{2}\frac{1}{|S\nabla g|^{2}}\partial^{i}(b_{i}\partial^{i}|S\nabla g|^{2})=\boxed{M2}+T_{M3}+\boxed{E3}+T_{E4},

where

\displaystyle\boxed{M2}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\sum_{i=1}^{2}\frac{2b_{i}}{|S\nabla g|^{2}}\langle\partial^{i}\nabla_{R}g,B\partial^{i}\nabla_{R}g\rangle,

\displaystyle T_{M3}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\frac{2}{|S\nabla g|^{2}}\left\langle\sum_{i=1}^{2}\partial^{i}\left(b_{i}\partial^{i}\nabla_{R}g\right),B\nabla_{R}g\right\rangle,

\displaystyle\boxed{E3}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\sum_{i=1}^{2}\frac{2b_{i}}{|S\nabla g|^{2}}\langle\partial^{i}\nabla_{R}g,\partial^{i}B\nabla_{R}g\rangle,\text{ and}

\displaystyle T_{E4}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\sum_{i=1}^{2}\frac{1}{|S\nabla g|^{2}}\partial^{i}\left(b_{i}\langle\nabla_{R}g,\partial^{i}B\nabla_{R}g\rangle\right).

All together we have

\displaystyle\operatorname{div}\left(A^{T}\nabla\log|S\nabla g|^{2}\right)=

\displaystyle\ T_{3.A}+\boxed{3.B}+\boxed{3.C}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \boxed{M1}+\boxed{M2}+T_{M3}

(Main terms)

\displaystyle+\boxed{3.B}+\boxed{3.C}+\boxed{E1}+\boxed{E2}+\boxed{E3}+T_{E4},

(Error terms)

and the first part of the lemma follows taking

T_{E}\coloneqq\boxed{3.B}+\boxed{3.C}+\boxed{E1}+\boxed{E2}+\boxed{E3}+T_{E4}.

Let us see now the second part of the lemma. Indeed,
\boxed{E2}
is
{\mathcal{O}}(1)
because
|\langle\nabla_{R}g,\partial^{i}B\nabla_{R}g\rangle|\lesssim|\nabla g|^{2}
. By (
7.20
) we get that
\boxed{3.B}
and
\boxed{3.C}
are in
{\mathcal{O}}\left(1+\frac{|\nabla^{2}g|}{|\nabla g|}\right)
, and (
7.3
) implies that
\boxed{E1}
and
\boxed{E3}
are of the form
{\mathcal{O}}\left(1+\frac{|\nabla^{2}g|}{|\nabla g|}\right)
. All in all,

T_{E}=T_{E4}+{\mathcal{O}}\left(1+\frac{|\nabla^{2}g|}{|\nabla g|}\right),

as claimed.∎

7.5.4.
Proof of
Lemma

7.16

In this subsection we prove that the two main terms cancel out in the sense

\left|\boxed{M1}+\boxed{M2}\right|\lesssim 1+\frac{|\nabla^{2}g|}{|\nabla g|}.

This cancellation is related to what happens in the constant case, see
Remark

7.15
.

We will see that they both have a common term in opposite sign, and exploiting this cancellation we will obtain a sum of error terms. The key identity is (
7.17
), which only applies in the plane.

The key idea to prove the lemma is that using (
7.5
), which relates
\partial^{1,2}g
and
\partial^{2,1}g
, and (
7.17
), which relates
b_{1}\partial^{1,1}g
and
b_{2}\partial^{2,2}g
, we will be able to write
\partial^{1,1}
,
\partial^{2,2}
,
\partial^{1,2}
and
\partial^{2,1}
in terms of
\partial^{1,1}
and
\partial^{2,1}
only.

We start by studying the term
\boxed{M1}\coloneqq-\sum_{i}\frac{4b_{i}}{|S\nabla g|^{4}}\langle\partial^{i}\nabla_{R}g,B\nabla_{R}g\rangle^{2}
in
\widetilde{\Omega}_{\varphi}
. Expanding the numerator,

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{2}b_{i}\langle\partial^{i}\nabla_{R}g,B\nabla_{R}g\rangle^{2}

\displaystyle=b_{1}\langle\partial^{1}\nabla_{R}g,B\nabla_{R}g\rangle^{2}+b_{2}\langle\partial^{2}\nabla_{R}g,B\nabla_{R}g\rangle^{2}

\displaystyle=b_{1}(b_{1}\partial^{1}g\partial^{1,1}g+b_{2}\partial^{2}g\lx@nobreakspace\partial^{1,2}g)^{2}+b_{2}(b_{1}\partial^{1}g\partial^{2,1}g+b_{2}\partial^{2}g\lx@nobreakspace\partial^{2,2}g)^{2}.

By (
7.5
) and (
7.17
), which read as
\partial^{1,2}g=\partial^{2,1}g+{\mathcal{O}}(|\nabla g|)
and
b_{2}\partial^{2,2}g=-b_{1}\partial^{1,1}g+{\mathcal{O}}(|\nabla g|)
respectively, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{2}b_{i}\langle\partial^{i}\nabla_{R}g,B\nabla_{R}g\rangle^{2}=

\displaystyle\ b_{1}(b_{1}\partial^{1}g\partial^{1,1}g+b_{2}\partial^{2}g\partial^{2,1}g+{\mathcal{O}}(|\nabla g|^{2}))^{2}

\displaystyle+b_{2}(b_{1}\partial^{1}g\partial^{2,1}g-b_{1}\partial^{2}g\partial^{1,1}g+{\mathcal{O}}(|\nabla g|^{2}))^{2}.

Expanding, and using
b_{1}(\partial^{1}g)^{2}+b_{2}(\partial^{2}g)^{2}=\langle\nabla_{R}g,B\nabla_{R}g\rangle=|S\nabla g|^{2}
and the cancellation of cross terms, we get

\sum_{i=1}^{2}b_{i}\langle\partial^{i}\nabla_{R}g,B\nabla_{R}g\rangle^{2}=|S\nabla g|^{2}\left(b_{1}^{2}(\partial^{1,1}g)^{2}+b_{1}b_{2}(\partial^{2,1}g)^{2}\right)+(\partial^{1,1}g+\partial^{2,1}g){\mathcal{O}}(|\nabla g|^{3})+{\mathcal{O}}(|\nabla g|^{4}).

In conclusion,
[formula]
is of the form

(7.27)

\boxed{M1}=-4\frac{b_{1}^{2}(\partial^{1,1}g)^{2}+b_{1}b_{2}(\partial^{2,1}g)^{2}}{|S\nabla g|^{2}}+\frac{{\mathcal{O}}(\partial^{1,1}g+\partial^{2,1}g)}{|S\nabla g|}+{\mathcal{O}}(1).

With the same strategy we study the term
\boxed{M2}\coloneqq\sum_{i}\frac{2b_{i}}{|S\nabla g|^{2}}\langle\partial^{i}\nabla_{R}g,B\partial^{i}\nabla_{R}g\rangle
in
\widetilde{\Omega}_{\varphi}
. Using (
7.5
) and (
7.17
) as before and expanding, the numerator can be written as

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{2}

\displaystyle b_{i}\langle\partial^{i}\nabla_{R}g,B\partial^{i}\nabla_{R}g\rangle=b_{1}^{2}(\partial^{1,1}g)^{2}+b_{1}b_{2}(\partial^{1,2}g)^{2}+b_{1}b_{2}(\partial^{2,1}g)^{2}+b_{2}^{2}(\partial^{2,2}g)^{2}

\displaystyle=b_{1}^{2}(\partial^{1,1}g)^{2}+b_{1}b_{2}\left(\partial^{2,1}g+{\mathcal{O}}(|\nabla g|)\right)^{2}+b_{1}b_{2}(\partial^{2,1}g)^{2}+\left(-b_{1}\partial^{1,1}g+{\mathcal{O}}(|\nabla g|)\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=2\left(b_{1}^{2}(\partial^{1,1}g)^{2}+b_{1}b_{2}(\partial^{2,1}g)^{2}\right)+\left(\partial^{1,1}g+\partial^{2,1}g\right){\mathcal{O}}(|\nabla g|)+{\mathcal{O}}(|\nabla g|^{2}).

Hence,
[formula]
is of the form

(7.28)

\boxed{M2}=4\frac{b_{1}^{2}(\partial^{1,1}g)^{2}+b_{1}b_{2}(\partial^{2,1}g)^{2}}{|S\nabla g|^{2}}+\frac{{\mathcal{O}}(\partial^{1,1}g+\partial^{2,1}g)}{|S\nabla g|}+{\mathcal{O}}(1).

Note that both
[formula]
in (
7.27
) and
[formula]
in (
7.28
) have the term

4\frac{b_{1}^{2}(\partial^{1,1}g)^{2}+b_{1}b_{2}(\partial^{2,1}g)^{2}}{|S\nabla g|^{2}}

in common with opposite sign, which allows us to remove the square in (
7.15
). That is, adding up both terms,

\boxed{M1}+\boxed{M2}=\frac{{\mathcal{O}}(\partial^{1,1}g+\partial^{2,1}g)}{|S\nabla g|}+{\mathcal{O}}(1),

whence we obtain

\left|\boxed{M1}+\boxed{M2}\right|\overset{\text{{(\ref{two directional derivatives})}}}{\lesssim}1+\frac{|\nabla^{2}g|}{|\nabla g|},

as claimed.
∎

7.5.5.
Proof of
Lemma

7.17

We study, for
\psi\in W^{1,\infty}_{c}(\widetilde{\Omega}_{\varphi})
, the term

T_{M3}(\psi)\coloneqq\left(\frac{2}{|S\nabla g|^{2}}\left\langle\sum_{i=1}^{2}\partial^{i}\left(b_{i}\partial^{i}\nabla_{R}g\right),B\nabla_{R}g\right\rangle\right)(\psi).

In this subsection we don’t use the fact that we are in the plane. Instead, the key ingredient is the
L_{A}
-harmonicity of the Green function far from the pole, and so the computations in this subsection could be done also in higher dimensions.

The term
T_{M3}
must be read as

(7.29)

\displaystyle T_{M3}

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{|S\nabla g|^{2}}\left\langle\sum_{i=1}^{2}\begin{pmatrix}\partial^{i}(b_{i}\partial^{i,1}g)\\
\partial^{i}(b_{i}\partial^{i,2}g)\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}b_{1}\partial^{1}g\\
b_{2}\partial^{2}g\end{pmatrix}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{|S\nabla g|^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{2}\left\{\partial^{i}(b_{i}\partial^{i,1}g)b_{1}\partial^{1}g+\partial^{i}(b_{i}\partial^{i,2}g)b_{2}\partial^{2}g\right\}

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{|S\nabla g|^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{2}\sum_{j=1}^{2}\partial^{i}(b_{i}\partial^{i,j}g)b_{j}\partial^{j}g\eqqcolon\sum_{i=1}^{2}\sum_{j=1}^{2}T_{M3}^{(i,j)}.

Here, for each
i,j\in\{1,2\}
,

(7.30)

T_{M3}^{(i,j)}(\psi)=T_{\partial^{i}(b_{i}\partial^{i,j}g)}\left(\frac{2}{|S\nabla g|^{2}}b_{j}\partial^{j}g\psi\right).

We want to study the functionals
T_{M3}^{(i,j)}
for
i,j\in\{1,2\}
. First note that

\frac{2}{|S\nabla g|^{2}}b_{j}\partial^{j}g\psi\in W_{c}^{1,\infty}(\widetilde{\Omega}_{\varphi}),

while
b_{i}\partial^{i,j}g\in L^{2}(\widetilde{\Omega}_{\varphi})
. Hence, (
7.30
) makes sense and it suffices to study the functionals
T_{\partial^{i}(b_{i}\partial^{i,j}g)}\in W_{c}^{1,\infty}(\widetilde{\Omega})^{\prime}
. In fact, fixed
j\in\{1,2\}
, we will exploit the cancellation of the functional
\sum_{i=1}^{2}T_{\partial^{i}(b_{i}\partial^{i,j}g)}
. For simplicity we will write
\partial^{i}(b_{i}\partial^{i,j}g)
instead of
T_{\partial^{i}(b_{i}\partial^{i,j}g)}
.

Compared to the strategy seen in
Remark

7.15
, now the matrix is not constant and hence the directional derivatives do not commute. For this reason, some error terms will appear in the procedure of extracting the gradient
\nabla_{R}
outside from

(7.31)

\sum_{i=1}^{2}\partial^{i}\left(b_{i}\partial^{i}\nabla_{R}g\right)=\begin{pmatrix}\partial^{1}(b_{1}\partial^{1,1}g)+\partial^{2}(b_{2}\partial^{2,1}g)\\
\partial^{1}(b_{1}\partial^{1,2}g)+\partial^{2}(b_{2}\partial^{2,2}g)\end{pmatrix},

as it is done in the constant matrix case in (
7.16
). The idea is the same as in the proof of
Lemma

7.16
, see
Section

7.5.4
. That is, using the ‘almost’-commutative property (
7.5
) (relating
\partial^{1,2}g
and
\partial^{2,1}g
) of the directional derivatives, and its functional version in (
7.10
), we manage to extract the gradient
\nabla_{R}
outside.

Let us fix
j\in\{1,2\}
. If
i=j
(clearly
\partial^{i,j}=\partial^{j,i}
in this case), then

(7.32)

\partial^{i}(b_{i}\partial^{i,j}g)=\partial^{j}\left(\partial^{i}(b_{i}\partial^{i}g)\right)-\partial^{i}(\partial^{j}b_{i}\partial^{i}g),

and when
i\not=j
, using (
7.5
) and (
7.10
) we get

(7.33)

\displaystyle\partial^{i}(b_{i}\partial^{i,j}g)\overset{\text{{(\ref{derivative_commutator})}}}{}

\displaystyle{\displaystyle=}\partial^{i}\left(b_{i}\partial^{j,i}g\right)+\partial^{i}\left(b_{i}(\partial^{i}R_{j}-\partial^{j}R_{i})\nabla g\right)

\displaystyle=\partial^{i}\left(\partial^{j}(b_{i}\partial^{i}g)\right)-\partial^{i}\left(\partial^{j}b_{i}\partial^{i}g\right)+\partial^{i}\left(b_{i}(\partial^{i}R_{j}-\partial^{j}R_{i})\nabla g\right)

\displaystyle\overset{\text{{(\ref{directional derivatives does not commute distribution})}}}{}

\displaystyle{\displaystyle=}\partial^{j}\left(\partial^{i}(b_{i}\partial^{i}g)\right)-(\partial^{j}R_{i}-\partial^{i}R_{j})\nabla(b_{i}\partial^{i}g)-\partial^{i}\left(\partial^{j}b_{i}\partial^{i}g\right)+\partial^{i}\left(b_{i}(\partial^{i}R_{j}-\partial^{j}R_{i})\nabla g\right).

Plugging (
7.32
) and (
7.33
) in (
7.31
), we get, for
j=1
,

(7.34)

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{2}\partial^{i}(b_{i}\partial^{i,1}g)=

\displaystyle\partial^{1}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{2}\partial^{i}\left(b_{i}\partial^{i}g\right)\right)-\sum_{i=1}^{2}\partial^{i}(\partial^{1}b_{i}\partial^{i}g)-(\partial^{1}R_{2}-\partial^{2}R_{1})\cdot\nabla(b_{2}\partial^{2}g)

\displaystyle+\partial^{2}\left(b_{2}(\partial^{2}R_{1}-\partial^{1}R_{2})\cdot\nabla g\right),

and for
j=2
,

(7.35)

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{2}\partial^{i}(b_{i}\partial^{i,2}g)=

\displaystyle\partial^{2}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{2}\partial^{i}\left(b_{i}\partial^{i}g\right)\right)-\sum_{i=1}^{2}\partial^{i}(\partial^{2}b_{i}\partial^{i}g)-(\partial^{2}R_{1}-\partial^{1}R_{2})\cdot\nabla(b_{1}\partial^{1}g)

\displaystyle+\partial^{1}\left(b_{1}(\partial^{1}R_{2}-\partial^{2}R_{1})\cdot\nabla g\right).

Note that in both cases there is the term
\sum_{i=1}^{2}\partial^{i}\left(b_{i}\partial^{i}g\right)
, which is studied in
Claim

7.20
. By (
7.29
) and using
Claim

7.20
in both (
7.34
) and (
7.35
), in the dual space we get

(7.36)

\displaystyle T_{M3}=

\displaystyle\frac{2}{|S\nabla g|^{2}}\sum_{j=1}^{2}b_{j}\partial^{j}g\left(\sum_{i=1}^{2}\partial^{i}(b_{i}\partial^{i,j}g)\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{2}\frac{2b_{j}\partial^{j}g}{|S\nabla g|^{2}}\left\{\partial^{j}\left(-\sum_{i=1}^{2}b_{i}\partial^{i}g\operatorname{div}R_{i}^{T}-\sum_{i,k=1}^{2}\partial_{i}\left(\frac{a_{ik}-a_{ki}}{2}\right)\partial_{k}g\right)-\sum_{i=1}^{2}\partial^{i}(\partial^{j}b_{i}\partial^{i}g)\right\}

\displaystyle+\frac{2b_{1}\partial^{1}g}{|S\nabla g|^{2}}\left\{-(\partial^{1}R_{2}-\partial^{2}R_{1})\cdot\nabla(b_{2}\partial^{2}g)+\partial^{2}\left(b_{2}(\partial^{2}R_{1}-\partial^{1}R_{2})\cdot\nabla g\right)\right\}

\displaystyle+\frac{2b_{2}\partial^{2}g}{|S\nabla g|^{2}}\left\{-(\partial^{2}R_{1}-\partial^{1}R_{2})\cdot\nabla(b_{1}\partial^{1}g)+\partial^{1}\left(b_{1}(\partial^{1}R_{2}-\partial^{2}R_{1})\cdot\nabla g\right)\right\}.

As a consequence of this, the right-hand equality in
Claim

7.20
and
|\nabla\partial^{i}g|={\mathcal{O}}(|\nabla g|)+{\mathcal{O}}(|\nabla^{2}g|)
, see (
7.3
), we conclude that the functional
T_{M3}
is of the form

T_{M3}=\sum_{j=1}^{2}\frac{2b_{j}\partial^{j}g}{|S\nabla g|^{2}}\left({\mathcal{O}}(|\nabla g|)+{\mathcal{O}}(|\nabla^{2}g|)+\partial^{1}\left({\mathcal{O}}(|\nabla g|)\right)+\partial^{2}\left({\mathcal{O}}(|\nabla g|)\right)\right),

as claimed.
∎

7.5.6.
Proof of
Lemma

7.18

First, we need the following claim, which we will prove later.

Claim 7.27
.

Let
\psi\in W^{1,\infty}_{c}(\widetilde{\Omega}_{\varphi})
. Then, for
i,j\in\{1,2\}
, both

T_{\frac{1}{|S\nabla g|^{2}}\partial^{i}\left({\mathcal{O}}(|\nabla g|^{2})\right)}(\psi)\text{ and }T_{\frac{b_{j}\partial^{j}g}{|S\nabla g|^{2}}\partial^{i}\left({\mathcal{O}}(|\nabla g|)\right)}(\psi)

are of the form

\int\frac{{\mathcal{O}}(|\nabla^{2}g|)}{|\nabla g|}\psi+\int{\mathcal{O}}(1)\psi+\int{\mathcal{O}}(1)\partial^{i}\psi.

Granted this, by
Lemma

7.17
and (
7.14
) we have

|T_{M3}(\psi_{\varepsilon}\varphi g)|+|T_{E}(\psi_{\varepsilon}\varphi g)|\lesssim\int\frac{|\nabla^{2}g|}{|\nabla g|}\psi_{\varepsilon}\varphi g+\int\psi_{\varepsilon}\varphi g+\int|\partial^{i}(\psi_{\varepsilon}\varphi g)|.

The second term in the right-hand side is bounded by a constant times
{\mathcal{H}}^{2}(\Omega)<\infty
as
\Omega
is bounded and
g\lesssim 1
in
\operatorname{supp}\varphi
, see (
W6
). Hence, it suffices to prove

\int\left|\partial^{i}(\psi_{\varepsilon}\varphi g)\right|\lesssim 1,\text{ for }i\in\{1,2\}.

By the product derivative rule and the notation
\partial^{i}f=R_{i}\cdot\nabla f
, we can use

\int\left|\partial^{i}\left(\psi_{\varepsilon}\varphi g\right)\right|\lesssim\int g|\nabla\varphi|+\int_{\operatorname{supp}\varphi}|\nabla g|+\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{\operatorname{supp}\nabla\psi_{\varepsilon}}g.

Since
|\nabla\varphi|\lesssim 1
and
g(x)\lesssim 1
in
\operatorname{supp}\varphi
(see (
W6
)), we get
\int g|\nabla\varphi|\lesssim 1
. For the second integral on the right-hand side we sum over Whitney cubes in
W_{\varphi}
and apply
items

5
and
1
in
Lemma

7.26

\int_{\operatorname{supp}\varphi}|\nabla g|\leq\sum_{Q\in W_{\varphi}}\int_{Q}|\nabla g|\overset{\text{{(\ref{sum over whitney region p3})}}}{\lesssim}\sum_{Q\in W_{\varphi}}\ell(Q)\cdot\widetilde{\omega}_{T}(50Q)\overset{\text{{(\ref{sum over whitney region p1})}}}{\lesssim}1.

On the other hand, using
\operatorname{supp}\nabla\psi_{\varepsilon}\subset U_{3\varepsilon}(\partial\widetilde{\Omega})
and
Lemma

7.26
(
3
) respectively, we have

\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{\operatorname{supp}\nabla\psi_{\varepsilon}}g\leq\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{U_{3\varepsilon}(\partial\widetilde{\Omega})}g\lesssim\varepsilon\leq 1,

and
Lemma

7.18
follows.∎

We now turn to the proof of
Claim

7.27
.

Proof of
Claim

7.27
.

First we study
T_{\frac{1}{|S\nabla g|^{2}}\partial^{i}\left({\mathcal{O}}(|\nabla g|^{2})\right)}(\psi)
. We have

\displaystyle T_{\frac{1}{|S\nabla g|^{2}}\partial^{i}({\mathcal{O}}(|\nabla g|^{2}))}(\psi)

\displaystyle=T_{\partial^{i}({\mathcal{O}}(|\nabla g|^{2}))}\left(\frac{1}{|S\nabla g|^{2}}\psi\right)

\displaystyle{=}-\int{\mathcal{O}}(|\nabla g|^{2})\frac{1}{|S\nabla g|^{2}}\psi\operatorname{div}R_{i}^{T}-\int{\mathcal{O}}(|\nabla g|^{2})\partial^{i}\left(\frac{1}{|S\nabla g|^{2}}\psi\right).

The first term in the right-hand side is of the form
\int{\mathcal{O}}(1)\psi
. Let us study the second term in the right-hand side. By (
7.19
),

\partial^{i}\left(\frac{1}{|S\nabla g|^{2}}\psi\right)=-\frac{2\langle\partial^{i}\nabla_{R}g,B\nabla_{R}g\rangle}{|S\nabla g|^{4}}\psi-\frac{\langle\nabla_{R}g,\partial^{i}B\nabla_{R}g\rangle}{|S\nabla g|^{4}}\psi+\frac{\partial^{i}\psi}{|S\nabla g|^{2}},

and hence we get

\displaystyle-\int{\mathcal{O}}(|\nabla g|^{2})\partial^{i}\left(\frac{1}{|S\nabla g|^{2}}\psi\right)

\displaystyle=\int\frac{{\mathcal{O}}(\partial^{i}\nabla_{R}g)}{|S\nabla g|}\psi+\int{\mathcal{O}}(1)\psi+\int{\mathcal{O}}(1)\partial^{i}\psi

\displaystyle{=}\int\frac{{\mathcal{O}}(|\nabla^{2}g|)}{|\nabla g|}\psi+\int{\mathcal{O}}(1)\psi+\int{\mathcal{O}}(1)\partial^{i}\psi.

Next we study
T_{\frac{b_{j}\partial^{j}g}{|S\nabla g|^{2}}\partial^{i}\left({\mathcal{O}}(|\nabla g|)\right)}(\psi)
, similarly as we did with the previous term. Now we have

\displaystyle T_{\frac{b_{j}\partial^{j}g}{|S\nabla g|^{2}}\partial^{i}({\mathcal{O}}(|\nabla g|))}(\psi)

\displaystyle=T_{\partial^{i}({\mathcal{O}}(|\nabla g|))}\left(\frac{b_{j}\partial^{j}g}{|S\nabla g|^{2}}\psi\right)

\displaystyle{=}-\int\frac{{\mathcal{O}}(|\nabla g|)b_{j}\partial^{j}g}{|S\nabla g|^{2}}\psi\operatorname{div}R_{i}-\int{\mathcal{O}}(|\nabla g|)\partial^{i}\left(\frac{b_{j}\partial^{j}g}{|S\nabla g|^{2}}\psi\right).

As before, the first term in the right-hand side is of the form
\int{\mathcal{O}}(1)\psi
and the second term is

-\int{\mathcal{O}}(|\nabla g|)\partial^{i}\left(\frac{b_{j}\partial^{j}g}{|S\nabla g|^{2}}\psi\right)=\int\frac{{\mathcal{O}}(1)\psi}{|S\nabla g|}\partial^{i}\left(b_{j}\partial^{j}g\right)+\int{\mathcal{O}}(|\nabla g|^{2})\partial^{i}\left(\frac{1}{|S\nabla g|^{2}}\right)\psi+\int{\mathcal{O}}(1)\partial^{i}\psi.

The claim follows by using (
7.3
) and (
7.19
) in the previous line.
∎
